<margin>Decorative fragment of marbled paper</margin>
<note>The binding features a fragment of marbled paper pasted over green cloth. Marbling is a method of aqueous surface design which can produce patterns similar to smooth marble or other kinds of stone. The specific "dendritic" or radiating circular patterns seen here were often created by the interaction of pigments and surfactants on a liquid surface before being transferred to paper.</note>

# — FOLIO 74 (verso). —

The motion of pendulums, when
they move with equal motion,
such pendulums will always <unclear>slowly, slowly</unclear>
come to join together, and in these
joinings, the one <unclear>slowly</unclear> the other,
the one <unclear>slowly</unclear> moves away from the other.

<margin>
from n . m . will be
<unclear>slowly, slowly</unclear>
distant from the
center . f .
</margin>

<margin>
remember <note>original: "mēbrā", likely a shorthand for "membrare" or "remember to note".</note>
</margin>

<insert>
this p...
will come to...
of equal ...
taken
</insert>

If one of these weights is always <unclear>given</unclear> motion with
equal velocity, the other that follows it will always
follow it with a different velocity; which velocity will be
so much greater as the following weight is closer
to the mover <note>The "mover" refers to the primary source of motion or the leading weight.</note>, and it will be so much less fast as that weight
is further away from said mover.

<term>pendulums</term> <note>original: "pēduli"; Leonardo uses this to describe swinging weights or oscillating mechanical parts.</term>, <term>motion</term> <note>original: "moto"; the physical movement or displacement of an object.</term>, <term>velocity</term> <note>original: "uelocità"; speed in a specific direction.</term>, <term>weight</term> <note>original: "peso"; the mass or load being moved.</term>, <term>mover</term> <note>original: "mouēte"; the component or force that initiates the movement.</term>, <term>center</term> <note>original: "cētro"; the fixed point of rotation or reference.</term>, <term>slowly</term> <note>original: "tarda"; indicating a decrease in speed or a slow rate of change.</term>

<summary>Leonardo explores the mechanics of motion, observing how the relative velocity between two moving weights or pendulums changes based on their distance from the source of motion. The page includes detailed sketches of pulleys, gears, and a large mechanical winch or press.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, pendulum, velocity, laws of motion, engineering sketches, Renaissance physics, pulleys, winch</keywords>

*The Son of the Stars*, a comedy in 3 acts, on March 19, 1891, at the evenings of the Rose + Cross <note>The "Salons de la Rose + Croix" were esoteric art exhibitions and performances organized by Péladan to promote Idealist art.</note>, and on Easter Sunday and Monday 1893, at the Palace of the Champ de Mars.

<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to Charles Ravaisson-Mollien into the general introduction of the work. # INTRODUCTION

*The Selected Texts of Leonardo da Vinci (1) have been received with favor; the French Academy awarded them a Charles Blanc prize, perhaps less for the aesthetic importance of this popularization than for the literary beauty of certain pages that truly equal the descriptions of the most famous writers, such as the Deluge and the Battle.* <note>Péladan refers to Leonardo’s vivid written descriptions of a cataclysmic flood and a chaotic battle scene, which showcase his mastery of prose as well as painting.</note>

*To reduce five thousand pages to three hundred and fifty-seven is necessarily to renounce many notable passages; it also obliges one to repeat the parts already known and which predecessors have rightly preferred.*

*A selection always has the disadvantage of leaving a doubt in the reader's mind; everyone mixes their own preferences and tendencies into their judgment. A complete, unabridged publication <note>original: "L’in-extenso"</note> in this case is impossible, less because of the length than because of the jumble.* <note>Leonardo’s notebooks are famously disorganized, containing shopping lists alongside profound scientific observations and artistic theories.</note>

*A page of Leonardo deserves the same veneration*

---

(1) <term>LEONARDO DA VINCI</term>. — **Selected Texts: Thoughts, Theories, Precepts, Fables, and Jokes**, translated in their entirety for the first time from the original manuscripts and arranged in methodical order with an introduction by <term>PÉLADAN</term>. <note>Joséphin Péladan (1858–1918) was an eccentric French novelist and Rosicrucian who played a major role in the 19th-century revival of interest in Leonardo.</note> Thirty-one facsimiles of drawings and sketches. Volume in-18 (5th edition) . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 3.50

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction, specifically contrasting the "Selected Texts" (a curated, organized volume) with the raw, chaotic reality of Leonardo's original manuscripts. It picks up from the previous page's assertion that every scrap of Leonardo's writing is a relic.</meta>

->### THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI<-

than a relic, even if it bears nothing more than a laundry list; however, theorems on the edge of a stone, the flight of an arrow, metal rolling, or even Latin declensions and conjugations, really offer no interest. <note>Péladan argues that while every scrap is historically precious, the technical and grammatical notes lack the "interest" of his greater theories when read in isolation.</note>

However, the *Selected Texts* do not convey the extraordinary spectacle of this most powerful brain and the grimoire <note>A book of magic spells or secret formulas; here used to describe the cryptic, almost supernatural complexity of Leonardo's notebooks.</note> he has left us.

*Grimoire* is the proper and precise word here; the succession of subjects is as astonishing as the way in which they are treated.

The engineer and the painter, the philosopher and the experimenter, the mechanic and the anatomist, the hydraulic engineer and the moralist, the geologist and the poet are juxtaposed to provide us with a portrait of the most astonishing mental activity that has ever existed

Leonardo, when presented methodically through a classification of thoughts into categories, does not provide a true likeness—any more than the Tower of Babel, before the confusion of tongues, would correspond to the biblical depiction. The character of this great spirit appears in the simultaneity of the most diverse preoccupations.

Are you familiar with the *sagoma*? <note>Italian for "template" or "profile," often referring to a tool used by masons or architects to ensure consistency in moldings.</note> It is a mason’s tool, a hand-scraper, unless one classifies it among trowels or ginning machines: the master dedicated pages to it, which are abruptly cut off by a remark such as this: “*It is at the extremities of bodies that grace reveals itself*” or by “*If your body were in accord with virtue, you would feel no desire in this world.*” <note>These sudden shifts from technical masonry to high aesthetics and moral philosophy are typical of Leonardo’s stream-of-consciousness writing style.</note> Then one stumbles upon the difference between the embrasures <note>original: "arbalétrières" — narrow vertical slits in a fortification wall for crossbowmen.</note> which are

<summary>Péladan argues that curated editions of Leonardo's work fail to capture his "grimoire-like" brilliance, which is best seen in the chaotic, simultaneous jumps between technical masonry and profound moral philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, manuscripts, grimoire, sagoma, mental activity, Tower of Babel, philosophy, architectural tools</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION XI

wide on the outside and those that are wide on the inside.

To whom, then, does such a <term>medley</term> <note>original: "bigarrure"; refers to a diverse, multicolored, or mismatched collection</note> appeal? As a whole, to no one; in its details, to everyone—be they a <term>spiritualist</term> <note>original: "spirite"; one who believes in communication with spirits</note>, a <term>squarer of the circle</term> <note>original: "chercheur de quadrature"; a person obsessed with the impossible mathematical problem of constructing a square with the same area as a given circle</note>, a member of the war council, or an <term>ophthalmologist</term> <note>an eye specialist</note>.

To compile the chosen "Texts," it was necessary to read all the published manuscripts in the hope of gleaning traits unnoticed by predecessors or disdained by them; and this reading formed three notebooks corresponding to the three major groups of manuscripts: the *Manuscripts of the Institute*, the *Windsor Manuscripts*, and the *Codex Atlanticus*. For a long time, there was hesitation in publishing these extracts, due to uncertainty regarding the public's receptivity.

For there could be no question of classifying each of the three collections philosophically: it was necessary, so to speak, to leaf through all these pages with the reader and cite the most characteristic ones. This work, born of a fanatical admiration, corresponds only to beings who profoundly admire the *Master of the Saint John* <note>A reference to Leonardo da Vinci, creator of the famous painting of St. John the Baptist</note>. One can be a very honest man without devotion and honor *Leonardo* without wishing to study him down to the confused details of his scattered work. But an era that groups select men under the invocation of a *Stendhal* <note>The French novelist known for his psychological depth and devotion to Italian art</note> certainly contains *Leonardians*, for whom the contemplation of this incomparable intelligence is a pleasure, all the more vivid because it requires a sort of spiritual <term>aristia</term> <note>original: "aristie"; a term derived from Ancient Greek referring to a hero's finest moment or state of excellence</note>. Finally, whatever judgment is passed on this publication, it claims no merit except that of intention.

Translating this old Italian presents no diffi—

<summary>The author explains the methodology of selecting Leonardo da Vinci's writings from major codices and identifies the intended audience as dedicated "Leonardians" capable of appreciating his complex, scattered genius.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Codex Atlanticus, Windsor Manuscripts, manuscript study, translation methodology, Stendhal, intellectual excellence</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction, transitioning from the selection of texts to the physical difficulty of reading Leonardo's mirror writing and the translator's personal reflections on Leonardo's scientific versus artistic legacy.</meta>

<page-num>XII</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

*difficulty, the same cannot be said for reading it: those who have deciphered this text—written backwards, that is to say, from right to left in the Hebrew fashion, with faded ink, cramped handwriting, and uncertain punctuation—as Mr. Ravaisson-Mollien did, have accomplished a feat of strength, even when using a mirror to set the text upright.*

*Must it be confessed that, like the people of the sixteenth century, the translator was drawn by the beauty of the drawings; the scientist astonished him without seducing him; that, secondly, he sought to rediscover the philosophy of Leonardo da Vinci, his metaphysical and moral doctrine; and that, finally, he believes this incomparable man wasted his time, squandering the most beautiful genius on inventing ribbon looms, rolling mills, dredgers, and cannons.*

*For such tasks, there are always people available in every age, whereas since May 2, 1519, no hand has drawn like the one that grew cold and still at the Château of Cloux, near Amboise <note>Now known as Clos Lucé, the manor house where Leonardo spent his final years</note>. Even for a genius, the day has only twenty-four hours; and the powers of application, however exalted, do not cross their limits.*

*The present compilation is equivalent to an original work in terms of time and the strain on the eyes and hand; and Leonardo labored as much or more to describe the <term>sagoma</term> <note>original: "sagoma"; an Italian term used by Leonardo referring to a template, mold, or the profile of a molding</note> as to draw an immortal sketch. He was therefore wrong to ask himself what movement must be given to the mobile plane to obtain an <term>ellipse</term>, and if the lever is double the counter-lever, and if it amounts to the same thing for the motor to have the weight in the middle of the lever as at the end of the counter-lever.*

<summary>The translator describes the immense physical challenge of deciphering Leonardo's mirror writing and expresses a controversial view that Leonardo's scientific and engineering pursuits were a waste of his unique artistic genius.</summary>
<keywords>mirror writing, Ravaisson-Mollien, Château of Cloux, Amboise, engineering vs art, sagoma, mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction, moving from the translator's personal critique of Leonardo's scientific diversions to a historical account of how the manuscripts were preserved, inherited, and eventually published.</meta>

<page-num>XIII</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>
<sig>2</sig>

-> "If the spleen is full and doubled against the opposite side, it causes as much pain and weight with half the spleen as in the women of the shadow it relieves (63 r.)." <note>original: "Sella liena edoppina alla contro allieva tonto fa altmalore evere ilpeso con meza la laliena quanta nell tonnine della chonbra allieva"; this is a transcription of Leonardo's mirror-writing in old Italian, likely a medical or anatomical observation.</note> <-

The manuscripts of *Leonardo* have their own history. We find the first mention of them from the pen of a secretary to the Cardinal of Aragon, who came to Cloux <note>The Château of Clos Lucé in France</note> on October 18, 1516. "...*Leonardo* has also written on the nature of water. Of various machines and other things, he has filled an infinite number of volumes, all written in the common tongue <note>original: "langue vulgaire"; referring to Italian rather than Latin</note> and which, when published, will be of the greatest utility and the most delightful charm."

By his will, he gave to *Francesco de Melzi*, "a gentleman of Milan, to thank him for the services he has rendered him in the past, all and each of the books that the testator possesses and other instruments and drawings concerning his art and the profession of painter."

In 1519, Melzi left the Château of Cloux—the master's death having occurred on May 2nd—and retired to Vaprio, taking the manuscripts with him. From them, he extracted what has been published under the name *Treatise on Painting*, a collection of pieces scattered throughout all the notebooks, and to it he added the *Treatise on Light and Shadow*. Copies of this work circulated; Cellini <note>Benvenuto Cellini, the famous Renaissance goldsmith and sculptor</note> bought one for the price of fifteen gold crowns while he was in the service of Francis I; he lent it to Sebastiano Serli <note>An influential Italian Mannerist architect</note>. In an incomplete state, the *Treatise on Painting* was published for the first time in Paris, in 1651, and translated immediately.

Mr. Piot has provided an account from a certain Ambrosio Mazzenta; it dates from the first quarter of the seventeenth century.

<summary>This page traces the provenance of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts from his death at Cloux to their inheritance by Francesco Melzi and the eventual publication of the Treatise on Painting.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Francesco de Melzi, Treatise on Painting, manuscripts, provenance, Cardinal of Aragon, Benvenuto Cellini, Francis I, history of art</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical narrative of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, detailing their dispersal after they left the possession of Francesco Melzi's heirs.</meta>

<page-num>XIV</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

This Mazzenta <note>Ambrogio Mazzenta (1559–1635), a Barnabite monk and early chronicler of the manuscripts</note>, who was studying law in Pisa, met Lelio Gavardi d’Isola there, the tutor to the children of Horatio Melzi. This unworthy son <note>Horatio was the son of Francesco Melzi, Leonardo's loyal pupil</note> had put the drawings, books, and works of Leonardo in the attic in old crates. The tutor managed to get thirteen manuscripts given to him, which he hoped to sell to the Grand Duke Francesco; the latter died, and Gavardi d’Isola came to Pisa to study law under his relative Aldus Manutius <note>The younger Aldus Manutius, of the famous Venetian printing family</note>.

The Mazzenta of this account made Gavardi feel ashamed for having appropriated such precious things; he spoke so persuasively that the aforementioned Gavardi charged him with returning everything to Horatio Melzi.

> "The latter was astonished and made me a gift of the manuscripts, saying that for many years there had been many drawings by the same author in the rooms of his villa, under roofs in poor condition."

Mazzenta’s brothers boasted of what they possessed, and many people asked Horatio Melzi for drawings and pages by Leonardo; among them was Pompeo Aretino, the knight Leone <note>Pompeo Leoni (c. 1533–1608), a sculptor and collector who served the Spanish court</note>. The latter promised Horatio Melzi a seat in the Senate of Milan if he recovered the thirteen manuscripts; Sebastiano <note>One of the Mazzenta brothers</note> was made to return seven of them by the Mazzentas, who kept six for themselves.

One was given to Cardinal Borromeo (the *Treatise on Light and Shadow*, now at the Ambrosiana <note>The Biblioteca Ambrosiana in Milan</note>); the second to the painter Figgini; the third to Duke Emmanuel of Savoy; the other three fell to Pompeo Aretino, who made them into a large book (*Codex Atlanticus* <note>The largest collection of Leonardo's papers, so named for its atlas-like size</note>). His heir Calchi sold it to Arconati for 300 crowns.

<summary>This page recounts the neglect of Leonardo's manuscripts by Horatio Melzi and their subsequent dispersal through the hands of the Mazzenta brothers and Pompeo Leoni, leading to the creation of the Codex Atlanticus.</summary>
<keywords>Mazzenta, Horatio Melzi, Pompeo Leoni, Codex Atlanticus, provenance, Milan, Ambrosiana Library, manuscript history</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the provenance history of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, specifically tracking their movement from the Mazzenta brothers and Pompeo Leoni into various institutional collections, including the French seizure under Napoleon.</meta>

<page-num>XV</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

Of the thirteen manuscripts belonging to Mazzenta, ten came into the hands of Leoni <note>Pompeo Leoni, the sculptor who attempted to consolidate Leonardo's papers</note>.

Those belonging to the Duke of Savoy and to Figgini are lost; the one belonging to the Cardinal, given to the Ambrosiana <note>The Biblioteca Ambrosiana in Milan</note>, is the current Manuscript C of the Institute <note>The Institut de France in Paris</note>.

In 1637, Arconati <note>Galeazzo Arconati, who famously refused to sell the manuscripts to King Charles I of England</note> gave to the Ambrosiana the *Codex Atlanticus* (393 pages gathering 1600 sheets), as well as manuscripts A, B, E, F, G, H, I, L, M, of the Institute, and also the manuscript currently held by Marquis Trivulzio. K was given to the Ambrosiana by Orazio Archinti in 1674.

In 1796, by order of Bonaparte, Leonardo's manuscripts were sent to Paris. The *Codex Atlanticus* went to the National Library and the twelve others to the Institute. Venturi <note>Giovanni Battista Venturi, the first scholar to seriously study Leonardo's scientific work</note> labeled them from A to M. When, in 1815, the Austrian commissioner reclaimed Leonardo's manuscripts, the Codex was returned to him, but the twelve volumes at the Institute were not found <note>A fortunate "oversight" for France, as these twelve notebooks remain in Paris to this day</note>.

Mr. Léopold Delisle <note>A renowned French librarian and historian</note> has recovered the two volumes formed from sheets torn out by Libri <note>Guglielmo Libri, a mathematician and infamous book thief who stole thousands of manuscripts</note> and sold to Lord Ashburnham. They are marked ASH. I (26 pages, 25 of which were taken from Manuscript B) and ASH. II (68 pages, containing the principal chapters of the *Treatise on Painting*).

---

Charles Ravaisson-Mollien, in a labor of ten years (1880–1891), deciphered with a magnifying glass and a mirror <note>Leonardo wrote in "mirror script," from right to left</note> and published in six folio volumes of facsimiles the text with the French translation facing it. The very high price of this work makes it inaccessible to most, and not all libraries possess it.

<summary>This page details the complex provenance of Leonardo da Vinci's notebooks, tracing their journey from 17th-century Italian collectors to their seizure by Napoleon and their eventual cataloging by scholars like Venturi and Ravaisson-Mollien.</summary>
<keywords>Codex Atlanticus, Ambrosiana Library, Institut de France, Napoleon Bonaparte, manuscript theft, Guglielmo Libri, Charles Ravaisson-Mollien, mirror writing</keywords>

<page-num>XVI</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

possess them. These extracts offered to the public do not claim to be anything more than an index, intended to show a wide variety of minds the pages that will interest them.

The translation by Mr. Ravaisson <note>Charles Ravaisson-Mollien, who published the first complete facsimile edition of the Paris manuscripts between 1881 and 1891</note> is perfect; I have not followed it, as I did not aim for literal word-for-word accuracy as he did, and as he was required to do.

The fourteen manuscripts are arranged here in their chronological order.

If the <term>Leonardians</term> <note>Scholars and enthusiasts dedicated to the study of Leonardo da Vinci</note> find pleasure in this compilation, they will subsequently be offered: the <term>Codex Atlanticus</term> <note>The largest collection of Leonardo's drawings and writings, currently in Milan</note> and the Windsor manuscripts <note>The collection of anatomical and artistic drawings held in the Royal Collection at Windsor Castle</note>; this will constitute, in three ordinary volumes, an <term>enchiridion</term> <note>original: "enchiridion"; a concise handbook or manual</note> of the five thousand pages preserved from the greatest genius of modern times.

->PELADAN.<-

# MANUSCRIPT B

->WRITTEN AROUND 1490, THE PERIOD DURING WHICH THE MASTER WAS WORKING ON THE *Tiburio* <note>The crossing tower or lantern gallery of a cathedral</note> OF THE MILAN CATHEDRAL, THE SFORZA EQUESTRIAN STATUE, AND *The Last Supper*.<-

In the year 1490, he went to Pavia with *Francesco di Giorgio* <note>Francesco di Giorgio Martini (1439–1501), a renowned architect and engineer from Siena who collaborated with Leonardo on architectural consultations</note>.

*Ch. Ravaisson-Mollien* <note>Charles Ravaisson-Mollien, the scholar who first published photostatic editions of Leonardo's manuscripts in the late 19th century</note> assigns this year to the portrait of *Cecilia Gallerani*, the woman with the weasel <note>Original French: "la femme à la fouine." This refers to the famous painting known today as the *Lady with an Ermine*</note>, whereas *Uzielli* <note>Gustavo Uzielli, an important 19th-century biographer of Leonardo</note> places this portrait around 1486.

*Watercolor pods and fruits; their authenticity has been disputed.*

->3 v.<-

How aqua vitae <note>original: "eau-de-vie"; literally "water of life," a high-proof distilled spirit used as a solvent</note> absorbs colors and scents: if you wish to make azure <note>a bright blue pigment</note> put cornflowers into it, and then poppies.

One should paint Fame <note>the personification of Renown</note> covered in tongues instead of feathers, and in the shape of a bird.

If your body were in accordance with virtue, you would have no worldly desires.

You believe in fame and reputation <insert>elsewhere the end of this sentence is found</insert> (like bread in the hands of children).

I say that movement is caused by several points of support <term>points d’appui: the fulcrums or leverage points in a mechanical or biological system</term>.

Force results from the deflation of the muscles and the shortening of the same; and the nerves tighten as much as is required by the sensation <note>Leonardo often used "sentiment" to describe the neurological impulse or "animal spirits"</note> which passes through the hollow cords <note>Leonardo believed nerves were hollow tubes that carried sensory information</note>.

->4 r.<-

On the 28th of April, I received from Marchesino <margin>treasurer</margin> <note>Marchesino Stanga, a prominent official and treasurer at the court of Ludovico Sforza in Milan</note>

(of the Duke of Milan or in charge of payments)
103 livres and furthermore 12.....

A rather heavy figure, shaded, of a draped man near a sort of flaming armillary sphere <note>An armillary sphere is a model of objects in the sky, consisting of a spherical framework of rings; here, Leonardo describes it as a combustible device</note>.

This ball, when it is thrown, is extinguished, and the reeds which have a strip of lit linen penetrate it when it touches the ground, and ignite the powder surrounded by tow <note>coarse, broken fibers of flax or hemp</note> soaked in secondary turpentine <note>original: "térébenthine seconde"; likely a lower grade or second distillation of the resin</note>, and the rest wrapped in hemp soaked in turpentine <note>original: "therentine"</note>, oil, and pitch: let the hoops be few in number so that the flames have air, otherwise you will achieve nothing.

->4 v.<-

*First draft of a refutation of spiritualism and necromancy.*

There can be no sound without movement or percussion of air; nor percussion of air where there is no instrument; nor an incorporeal instrument. Consequently, a spirit has neither voice, nor form, nor strength; and if it takes on a body, it will not be able to penetrate where the doors are closed.

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

If one were to claim that by compressing air <note>theory of the astral body. Leonardo is unaware of the existence of fluids/gases in the modern chemical sense</note> the spirit assumes bodies of various forms; and that with their help it speaks and moves with force; I reply that where there are neither nerves nor bones, there can be no force or movement, such as those attributed to imaginary spirits.

Avoid the allegations of those speculators whose reasonings are not confirmed by experience <note>Leonardo frequently champions "Esperienza" (experience/experimentation) over abstract scholastic theory</note>.

*Theorems on the sagitta and the string of the stone <note>original: "la flèche et le fil de la pierre"; likely referring to geometric properties of an arch or dome</note> (first figure of a dome, study for the Tiburio <note>The "Tiburio" refers to the crossing tower or lantern of the Milan Cathedral, a project Leonardo worked on extensively</note>).*

*Figures of a ravelin <note>A triangular fortification located in front of the innercurtain wall</note> for a fortress, square tower.*

**FALARICA.** — A crank-operated machine launching a barbed javelin, a small crossbow <note>The Falarica was originally a heavy Roman throwing spear, here reimagined as a mechanical weapon</note>.

## 6 r.

(1) Falarica <note>A heavy Roman siege spear, often wrapped in combustible materials</note>, two mounts for launching the javelin.

Square enclosure <note>Referring to a fortified perimeter or courtyard</note>.

If you stop your ship and place the tube of a blowgun <note>original: "sarbacane"; here Leonardo suggests using a long tube as a primitive hydrophone</note> into the water and listen at the other end, you will hear ships coming, even from a great distance.

Method for emptying a port <note>Likely a reference to dredging or clearing silt from a harbor</note>.

## 6 v.

*Text on the nature of mirrors and various figures of machines that are difficult to describe.*

---

(1) The falarica is a very ancient javelin or spear; at Saguntum <note>An ancient city in Spain famous for a siege by Hannibal</note> it was launched with the help of a machine from the top of towers called *falae*: the name comes from these siege towers. Livy and Vegetius <note>Titus Livius and Flavius Vegetius Renatus, Roman historians and military theorists</note> speak of it. This javelin was launched while set on fire. In Leonardo’s time, the falarica was both a polearm and a firebrand. There are drawings of a dog and a cat armed with a falarica, which were released to set fire to the enemy camp.

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, Falarica, blowgun/listening tube, Livy, Vegetius, polearm, firebrand, spear</vocab>

### 7 r.

<term>RHOMPHAEA</term> <note>A heavy polearm or sword used by the Thracians</note> (1). — This rhomphaea cannot be used with <term>courtauds</term> <note>Short-statured horses or perhaps a specific type of short-barreled cannon; the context here suggests a mismatch in equipment size or cavalry type</note>.

### 7 v.

Again the rhomphaea and catapults.

The <term>scorpion</term> <note>A Roman torsion engine used for firing bolts</note> is a machine that can launch stones, darts, and arrows.
Other authors claim that the <term>scorpion</term> is a poisoned arrow which, as soon as it touches the blood, brings death. It is said that this weapon was found among the Scythians <note>Ancient nomadic people of Central Asia</note>, others say in Candia <note>The historical name for Crete</note>. The mixture is made of human blood and serpent venom. One should only use this weapon against traitors, for it originates from them.

Sharp iron for wounding and restraining elephants. Livy <note>Titus Livius, a Roman historian</note> says that many elephants had been killed by their own drivers <note>original: "cornac"</note> because they had fallen into a frenzy: they are struck with the <term>scalpre</term> <note>A chisel-shaped tool or spike; see note on previous page</note> between the ears: this was the swiftest death that could be given to such a large beast.

**VERVINE.** — The vervine, according to what I find in a comedy by Plautus, is a javelin with a long handle.

**SOLIFERREUM.** — An iron weapon that was thrown at the first assault <note>A Roman-era heavy javelin forged entirely from iron</note>.

**SLING.** — Flavius Josephus says that it was invented by the inhabitants of the Balearic Islands. Mothers would not let their children touch any food other than that which had been knocked down from a target by them, using the stone of a sling: however, Pliny attributes the invention of the sling to the Syrophoenicians.

->9 v.<-

**AUCTEURS.** — According to Celidonius, a short scythe with a short handle, shaped like a swallow's tail, is carried unsheathed, attached to the belt.

**DANOIRES.** — Long hatchets. For bladed weapons, the edge tempered in oil becomes delicate, and brittle when tempered in water:

### Scythes.

**Fragilique.** — This is a globe half a foot in size, filled with paper tubes <note>functioning as fuses or small rockets</note> and coated with a paste of sulfur and <unclear>bark</unclear> (?): at the center of the globe, there is gunpowder. When it is thrown at the enemy with a sling, the fire first catches a fuse and the fragments spread over a distance of 100 braccia <note>An Italian unit of measurement (arms), roughly 23 inches; 100 braccia is approximately 60 meters</note>.

->10 r.<-

**Scythed chariot.** — (*A lovely illustration: the woman is at the hub of the wheels* <note>This likely refers to a decorative figurehead or a specific mechanical component in Leonardo's sketch of a chariot equipped with rotating blades</note>).

**Romphaia.** — Spears of three cubits <note>approx. 4.5 feet</note> bristling with points; they were thrown into the midst of the enemy using a cord attached to a stick, serving as a sling.

**Romphaia.** — These <term>rocaces</term> <note>Likely a variation of a hooked weapon or reaping tool</note> and romphaia <note>A long-bladed polearm or sword used by Thracian tribes</note> were employed by the Macedonians against the Tartars, who are accustomed to running throughout their entire lives.

### 11 r.

#### GREEK ATAMEGANTO (?) <note>The meaning of this heading is uncertain; it may be a corrupted Greek term or a specific alchemical recipe name</note>

If you wish to create a foul stench, take human excrement and urine; otherwise, take cabbages and... and place them in a tightly sealed jar for one month under manure, then break it wherever you wish to create the stench.

One can also take eels and urine, or crayfish, always in a jar under manure, and break it where you wish.

---

### 11 v.

*(Illustration of a forked spear).*
When it strikes and sinks in, the fire ignites a mixture contained within it, and the flaming liquid runs over the armor and enters through the joints of the breastplate <term>cuirasse: a piece of armor consisting of a breastplate and backplate fastened together</term>.

## *Two plans analogous to that of Bramante for St. Peter's in Rome <note>2</note>.*

->18 v.<-

The sentinels of fortresses must be chosen by lot every evening; let no one know who he shall have as a companion. In times of

---

<note>1</note> In 1488, concurrently with Bramante, Pietro di Gorgonzola, and Luca Paperio, the Master <note>Leonardo</note> competed for the dome of the Milan Cathedral; his model was not accepted; in 1491, he reclaimed it, having received 93 livres and 14 soldi in indemnity.

Mr. de Geymüller has studied Leonardo as an architect better than any other.

<note>2</note> Bramante, who arrived in Lombardy around 1474 while Leonardo did not arrive until 1483, created the facade of the Cathedral at Abbiategrasso in 1477, the facade of the Fontana house, the door of the Mazzonico house, and between 1479 and 1499, the church of San Satiro.

Leonardo's manuscript dates from 1490.

There would be reason to compare good photographs of Bramante's monuments prior to this date with Leonardo's drawings, except for the central projections. However, it was only ten years later, in 1500, that Bramante would begin his Roman works; Leonardo therefore discovered the "Bramantesque" style simultaneously with the man who was to be the master of works at the Vatican.

Apse <note>the semi-circular or polygonal recess at the end of a church</note> of a domed church (1).

---

(1) To gain an understanding of Leonardo as an architect, one must see the Vallardi collection at the Louvre Museum, specifically the mausoleum built on an artificial mound in the manner of the Chaldeans <note>an ancient people of Mesopotamia, modern-day Iraq</note>. A terrace with six doors provides access to three funerary halls: the artificial mound is crowned with a small Pantheon <note>a temple or circular building with a dome</note> open to the sky. It is quite strange that no one thought to execute this project when it came to the Walhalla <note>a hall of fame for distinguished Germans, actually located in Bavaria</note> in Thuringia, the monument to the dead at Père Lachaise <note>the famous cemetery in Paris</note>, or the construction for the House of Savoy in Rome. Leonardo created the landscape at the same time as the building: and for me, this inspiration dates from his journey to the East; he perhaps had this idea while standing before the Tell <term>tell: an archaeological mound formed by the ruins of ancient settlements</term> that was the Tower of Babel.

->30 r.<-

Flaming <term>Philacrote</term> <note>A historical term for a type of incendiary device</note>, <term>arzila</term>, <term>crusida</term>, <term>lampade</term> or <term>Greek fire</term> <note>A legendary incendiary weapon used by the Byzantine Empire that could burn even on water</note> is a ball furnished with spikes and which one throws: it contains a mixture of willow charcoal, saltpeter, brandy, sulfur, pitch, and camphor, with a thread of Ethiopian silk. Callimachus, an architect, was the first who taught it to the Romans. Leo, the Emperor, employed it when the Orientals came against Constantinople with an infinite number of ships which were all burned by this engine.

### 33 r.

The steam cannon <note>original: "Architonnerre"; a name Leonardo likely coined from 'arch' (Archimedes) and 'tonnerre' (thunder)</note> is a machine made of fine copper invented by Archimedes, which throws iron balls with great noise and fury. The third part of the instrument consists of a large charcoal fire: when it is well heated, tighten the screw located above the water vessel, and this vessel will open into the bottom. The water in the heated part of the instrument will transform into steam. This machine could throw a ball weighing one talent <note>A "talent" was an ancient unit of weight, roughly 60 to 75 pounds</note>.

Names of weapons.

Acinaces <note>original: "Acinace"</note>, a knife used by the Scythians and the Medes, according to Acron. <note>Acron was a 2nd-century commentator on Horace; the Scythians and Medes were ancient nomadic and Iranian peoples.</note>

It was called an *aclys* <note>original: "aclide"</note> because it is made for carnage.

*Spada*, *ensis*, *gladius*—names of weapons that are universally known, especially among the ancients. <note>Leonardo is listing various Latin and Italian terms for swords to categorize his studies of antiquity.</note>

*Baculus* <term>baculus: a simple staff or rod</term>, a stick without a hook with which unfortunate slaves were beaten.

*Conti* <note>original: "Concti"</note> <term>contus: a long, heavy pike or cavalry lance used without a throwing strap</term>, long and strong lances, without iron heads.

The *lancea* <term>lancea: a light spear or javelin, often equipped with a leather strap for throwing</term>, invented by the Italians according to Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder was a Roman author and naturalist.</note>. Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro was an ancient Roman scholar and writer.</note> says that the word is Spanish.

->43 r.<-

The *pilum* <term>pilum: the heavy javelin used by Roman legionaries, designed to bend upon impact</term>, a Roman spear; the *gaesum* <note>original: "gœstum"</note> <term>gaesum: a heavy iron javelin used by the Gauls</term> of the Gauls; and the *sarissa* <term>sarissa: a long pike, up to 18 feet, used in the Macedonian phalanx</term> of the Macedonians.

*Ruma*, *pilum*, *rumex* <term>rumex: a type of dart or javelin</term>, and *telum* <term>telum: a general Latin term for any missile weapon or spear</term>, which are consistent with one another, are similar to the Gallic *sparum* <term>sparum: a small hunting spear or rustic javelin</term>.

The javelin (*jaculum* <term>jaculum: a common throwing spear or dart</term>) invented by Aetolus <note>original: "Etale"; in mythology, Aetolus was credited with many inventions, though Leonardo notes him here as a son of Mars.</note>, son of Mars.

*Gabina* <term>gabina: a type of Illyrian spear</term>, an Illyrian weapon, a hunting spear.

*Clava* <term>clava: a wooden club or cudgel</term>, used by Hercules; a studded club which, in uncivilized times, could be considered a very noble weapon.

->44 r.<-

*Dolabra* <term>dolabra: a versatile Roman tool combining an axe-head and a pick, used for entrenching and demolition</term>, a broadaxe, with two edges.

*Bipennis* <note>original: "Bipenne"</note> <term>bipennis: a double-headed battle-axe</term>, sharpened on both sides.

Tools for excavators <note>Leonardo identifies these heavy axes as equipment for laborers and soldiers tasked with earthworks or trench-digging.</note>.

->44 v.<-

A pointed cross with four corners; it is thrown <note>This likely describes a caltrop, an anti-cavalry weapon thrown on the ground so that one point always faces upward.</note>

where it is born, it retains its weight and its volume; I call it ephemeral because it tends to overcome its cause, and once this cause is defeated, it dies. <note>Leonardo is likely describing 'force' or 'impetus' here, which he viewed as a temporary power imparted to an object that eventually exhausts itself.</note>

# ITALIAN MANUSCRIPT NO. 2038

## OF THE NATIONAL LIBRARY
### ACQUISITION 8070. LIBRI
### ASHBURNHAM PLACE

*The "Manuscript A" of the Institute <note>The Institut de France, one of the primary repositories for Leonardo da Vinci's scientific notebooks.</note> originally contained 114 leaves. Libri <note>Guglielmo Libri (1803–1869), a mathematician and inspector of French libraries who notoriously stole thousands of rare manuscripts.</note> removed the 54th leaf and the final 50 leaves (numbered 64 to 114). These are paginated at the top of the <term>rectos</term> <note>The front side of a leaf in a manuscript.</note>, on the right, in small numerals written in Leonardo’s own hand. These pages were sold to Lord Ashburnham and now constitute the 34 leaves numbered in red ink from 1 to 34.*

*Mr. Léopold Delisle <note>The head of the Bibliothèque Nationale (National Library) who successfully campaigned for the return of the stolen Libri manuscripts.</note> facilitated the return of this manuscript to the National Library, similar to the Ashburnham I manuscript, and the National Library has since returned them to the Library of the Institute.*

*The demonstration follows.*

<page-num>48</page-num>
# THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

Which is the most difficult: shadow, light, or good drawing?

I believe that the difficult part is that which is constrained by a limit <note>original: "terme"; Leonardo refers to the precise boundaries or "terms" that define a form</note> rather than that which is entirely free. Shadows have their limits at certain degrees; and for one who is ignorant of them, things are without relief; yet, relief is the importance and the soul of painting.

Drawing is free, considering that if you see infinite faces (*for an infinity*), they will all be different, one with a long nose or a short one.

Therefore the painter can, he too, take this liberty: where there is liberty, there is no rule.

->I V.<-

Every object moved with violence <note>In Renaissance physics, "violent motion" refers to movement caused by an external force, as opposed to "natural motion" like falling</note> will follow in the air the line of the movement of its mover.

If someone moves an object in a circle and it is released during that movement, that movement is curved; if the movement is begun in a circle and ends in a straight line, straight will be its course.

<vocab>shadow, light, drawing, relief, painting, movement, mover, straightness</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo discusses why mastering shadow is more difficult and essential than drawing due to its precise rules, and transitions into observations on the physics of projectile motion.</summary>
<keywords>shadow and light, relief in painting, artistic liberty, laws of motion, violent motion, physics of art</keywords>

->6 verso<-

### Visual pyramids. <note>The "visual pyramid" is a fundamental concept in Renaissance perspective, imagining lines of sight traveling from the object to a single point in the eye.</note>

For narrative paintings, the painter must consider the height of the plane where he places the figures, so that when he draws from nature he keeps his eye as much lower as the object is higher than the eye of the spectator; otherwise, the work would be reprehensible. <note>Leonardo is warning against "distorted perspective," where the artist's viewpoint doesn't match the intended viewing angle of the finished painting.</note>

Painters fall into despair at not achieving the relief and the liveliness of reality, which far exceeds in clarity and darkness the things seen in mirrors. They accuse themselves of ignorance.

It is impossible for a painted thing to have the same relief as a mirror, even though the glass and the canvas <note>The sentence breaks off here, likely continuing on the following page to explain the physical differences between a 2D surface and the depth perceived in a reflection.</note>

<summary>Leonardo advises painters on adjusting their physical perspective when drawing tall subjects and discusses the inherent difficulty in capturing the high-contrast "relief" and depth found in mirrors.</summary>

be equally flat. The base of the visual lines is so wide that one sees the second object behind the first: but if you close one eye, the visual line will originate from a single point, making its base at the first object, so that a second object of equal size is never seen. <note>Leonardo is explaining binocular vision—the way our two eyes see "around" an object—and why a flat painting, viewed with both eyes, can never perfectly mimic the three-dimensional depth of reality or a mirror.</note>

### Optics.

->10 r.<-

Colors, in the distance, within the shadows,
are unknown and imperceptible.
The painter must train his hand by copy-
ing drawings by a good master and, once said
habit is acquired, he must, with the advice of his

To draw from relief, the eye of the
model must be at the same height as the eye of the artist.
You shall do this for a head from nature,
for the passers-by you encounter all have
their eyes at the height of yours, and if you
made them either higher or lower, your portrait
would not be a likeness.

### Light and shadow.

->17 v.<-

The young artist shall first learn perspective, then the proportions <note>original: "mesures"</note>, then from the hand of a good master, to accustom himself to well-formed limbs; then from nature, to confirm the principles learned; then from the drawings of various masters, to acquire the habit of practice.

The first painting was the silhouette <note>original: "la silhouette"</note> which surrounded the shadow of a man on the wall. Draperies should be drawn from nature. Old men should be made nonchalant and with slow movements, the legs bent at the knees and the feet far apart, the spine curved, the head forward, and the arms little extended.

Women should be depicted in modest attitudes, their legs pressed together, their arms close to the body, and their heads lowered and tilted to the side.

Old women should be represented as bold, with brisk and angry movements, like infernal furies <note>In classical mythology, the Furies were female deities of vengeance known for their frantic energy.</note>; the movements of their arms should be more energetic than those of their legs.

Children should have lively and contorted movements when sitting; when standing, they should appear timid and fearful.

->18 r.<-

There are three types of perspective: first, regarding the reasons for the diminution <note>The mathematical reduction of size as objects recede.</note> of objects; second, regarding the fading of colors; third, regarding the degree of finish and clarity, according to the distance.

You shall reveal the near-true thickness of the limbs only for a nymph or an angel, who are represented under light garments, which are pushed and pressed against the body by the blowing of the winds.

### How painting surpasses all human works through the subtle speculation that belongs to it.

The eye, the window of the soul, is the primary way by which the common sense <note>In Renaissance psychology, the "common sense" (*sensus communis*) was considered a physical faculty in the brain where all sensory inputs—sight, sound, etc.—were gathered and judged.</note> can simply and magnificently contemplate the infinite works of Nature; the ear is the second, ennobling itself through the narration of what the eye has seen.

If you—historians, poets, or observers—had not seen with the eye, you could report nothing in your writings, which are born from painting <note>Leonardo argues that all written descriptions are merely derivative of visual experience, which he equates to "painting."</note>.

If you, poet, depict a story with the painting of the pen, the painter himself will do better with the brush. If you call painting "mute poetry," the painter will say of your writing that it is "blind painting." Which is worse: to be blind or mute?

If the poet is as free as the painter in his inventions, he cannot give as much satisfaction; for if poetry depicts words, the painter gives the actual likenesses of forms. What expresses the

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, painting, poetry, eye as the window of the soul, historians</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo argues for the supremacy of painting over poetry, famously describing the eye as the "window of the soul" and characterizing writing as "blind painting" compared to the visual truth of the brush.</summary>
<keywords>paragone, visual perception, common sense, painting vs. poetry, sensory hierarchy</keywords>

Which describes a man better, a word or a drawing? The name of a man changes according to the country, but the form remains the same everywhere until death.

### 19 v

If the poet serves the ear, the painter employs the eye, which is the worthier sense. If a good painter depicts the fury of a battle, and if a poet describes it, you will see where the onlookers bestow their highest praise. Write down the name of God and place His image beside it. You will see which one is more revered. Painting embraces all forms, whereas you <note>Addressing poets</note> have only names, which are not universal like forms; if you possess the effects of manifestations, we possess the manifestations of those effects.

Let a poet describe the beauty of a lady and let a painter paint her: you will see where Nature will lead the enamored judge.

Have you placed painting among the <term>mechanical arts</term> <note>In the Renaissance, "mechanical arts" were considered manual trades, inferior to the "liberal arts" like rhetoric or music</note>? Simply because it is performed by hand? But you write and you draw with the pen as well.

If a poet can say: "I will create a

<summary>Leonardo argues that painting is superior to poetry because the eye is a nobler sense than the ear and visual forms are universal, unlike shifting languages. He challenges the classification of painting as a lowly 'mechanical art' by pointing out that poets also use their hands to write.</summary>

<keywords>Paragone, superiority of painting, visual vs verbal, mechanical arts, universal language, aesthetics, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

If you were to say that poetry is eternal, I would answer that the works of a coppersmith last longer than ours or yours; nevertheless, they possess little imagination. Painting can be made eternal by painting on copper with glass colors <note>Likely referring to enamel work or oil painting on metal supports</note>. We, in art, may be called the grandchildren of God. If poetry deals with moral philosophy, painting deals with natural philosophy <note>The study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science</note>; if the former describes the actions of the mind, the latter considers the movements of the mind <note>Leonardo believed that physical gestures and facial expressions—"movements"—revealed the internal state of the soul</note>. Should the <term>poet</term> <note>original: "poëte"</note> set out to represent beauty, ferocity, or something execrable, ugly, and monstrous, or if he should perform a transformation of forms, will the painter not satisfy the viewer more?

->20 r.<-

If you despise painting, which is the sole imitator of all the visible works of nature, you despise a subtle invention which, with philosophical and subtle speculation, considers all the forms, qualities, positions, plants, animals, grasses, and flowers that are enveloped in shadow and light.

Painting is an art and a science, the legitimate daughter of nature, or, more correctly, the granddaughter of nature, because visible things were brought forth by nature, from which painting was born; therefore, it is rightly the granddaughter of nature and a relative of God.

### On Action.

You shall make figures in such an act as is sufficient to show what the figure has in its mind; otherwise, your art would not be praiseworthy. <note>Leonardo’s famous theory of "moti mentali"—that physical gestures must reveal the internal thoughts and emotions of the subject.</note>

If you have a courtyard suitable for being covered with a canvas: or else, have your model pose near evening, leaning against a wall.

Observe in the street, as evening approaches and when the weather is bad, how much grace and sweetness the faces have.

Therefore, painter, you should have a courtyard with black walls and a roof overhang over the wall; and when there is sun, you shall cover it with a canvas, or you shall paint the portrait at the evening hour, when there are clouds or fog: that air is perfect. <note>Leonardo preferred "sfumato" or soft, diffused lighting, which he found most flattering and realistic, avoiding the harsh shadows of direct sunlight.</note>

### How to depict a storm.

If you wish to represent a storm well, first consider and establish its effects: when the wind blows over the surface of the sea and the land, churning and carrying away everything that does not resist the universal tide. <note>Leonardo often viewed the movement of air and water as analogous; here "universal tide" refers to the overwhelming, rhythmic force of the wind acting like a sea current.</note> And to depict this storm well, you shall first make disordered and broken clouds, driven by the course of the wind, accompanied by an earthy dust risen from a seabed <note>original: "lit marin." Leonardo likely refers to sand or silt swept up from the shore by the gale.</note> and branches and leaves torn away by the tempest, scattered in the air along with many other light objects. The trees and grasses bent to the ground, as if to show they wish to follow the wind's course, with branches carried far from their natural place, and scattered leaves.

The men who are found there, some fallen and as if tangled in their clothes and in the dust, almost unrecognizable; and those who remain standing, clutching some tree so as not to be carried off by the wind; others, their hands over their eyes because of the dust, huddling toward the ground,

<note>This passage is a quintessential example of Leonardo's "procomponimento"—a written "pre-composition" where he uses vivid descriptive prose to visualize a scene before ever touching a brush to the panel.</note>

The sea, troubled and upheaved, shall be full of surging waves and foaming in its high swells; and the wind rises in the air, which is contested by the lighter foam, swirling in the manner of tangled clouds.

The ships that are there, some with sails torn, their tatters flapping in the wind along with their broken rigging; some masts snapped and fallen, with the ship caught broadside and shattered among the impetuous waters; certain men screaming, clinging to some debris of the vessel. You shall make the clouds driven by the impetuosity of the wind and beaten against the peaks of the mountains; and make them into confused whirlpools, just as the wave does when striking against the rocks. The air should be terrifying due to the darkness created in the atmosphere by dust, clouds, and thick mist.

->22 V.<-

I shall not fail to include, among these precepts, an invention which, although small and seemingly ridiculous, is useful for exciting the imagination. Look at a wall smeared with stains or made of mixed stones; you will see there landscapes, mountains, rivers, battles, and groups of figures; you will discover strange appearances of landscapes that you can then bring to a proper form.

It is the same with such a wall as it is with the sound of a bell, in which you will hear your own name or any word you might imagine.

Regarding the ten functions <term>offices: the specific duties or operations of a faculty</term> of the eye: brightness, darkness, body and color, shape and position, distance and proximity, movement and rest. It is from these functions that this little work of mine shall be woven, reminding the painter by what rule and in what manner he must imitate the work of nature—the ornament of the world—and the perspective of colors.

->23 r.<-

I provide the degrees of things presented to the eye, just as the musician provides the degrees of voices presented to the ear.

<page-num>65</page-num>

### 23 v.

# Optics.

### 24 r.

When you have drawn a thing many times, try to produce it without a model; on the other hand, have the model traced onto a pane of glass, and place this over the drawing you made without the model. Note carefully where your drawing does not align with the tracing. <note>This exercise was intended to train the artist’s "inner eye" and visual memory, a core part of Leonardo's pedagogy.</note>

If you wish to do well when drawing from a living model, fix a frame divided into squares between your eye and the model, and draw the same squares on your paper. Place a small ball of wax in a part of the mesh to serve as a sighting point that you will always align with the pit of the throat, the <term>fontanella: the hollow space at the base of the neck between the collarbones</term>. The threads of the grid will teach you the position of the parts of the body with every movement. <note>This device, often called a "veil" or *velum*, allowed artists to translate a three-dimensional subject onto a two-dimensional surface with mathematical precision.</note>

### 24 v.

## A Comparison of Painting and Sculpture.

The sculptor, if he creates his work in clay or wax, can both take away and add; once the work is finished, it is easily cast in bronze. <note>Leonardo is beginning his famous *Paragone*, or "comparison of the arts," where he weighs the merits of painting against sculpture.</note>

<page-num>68</page-num>

They <note>Referring to sculptors.</note> cannot represent transparent bodies, nor luminous ones, nor reflected forms, nor <term>lucid bodies<note>original: "corps lucides"; in Leonardo's terminology, these are objects that possess a polished, mirror-like surface that reflects light and images.</note></term> like mirrors and other shiny objects, nor clouds, nor darkness; and many natural effects are impossible for them.

Sculpture resists time better: so be it; although painting done on copper covered with white enamel and executed with enamel colors passed through fire and baked, is just as durable and even surpasses sculpture. One might say that if an error is made with this process, it is difficult to repair. It is a poor argument to claim that a process where retouching is impossible makes the work more worthy. I would say instead that the master's <term>invention<note>The initial intellectual design or conceptualization of the work.</note></term> is more difficult to correct if he makes such an error, than it is to correct the work *itself* thus spoiled.

->26 r.<-

I have found it useful, when lying in the darkness of the bed, to repeat to oneself the superficial outlines of the forms previously studied and other things, in order to strengthen the memory.

Certainly, one must not reject anyone's judgment while one is painting a man.

We know that everyone, even without being a painter, possesses an innate understanding of human forms and will judge well whether a figure is good, or if a shoulder is too high or too low, or if it has a large mouth, a large nose, or other defects.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's reflections on the artistic process, specifically focusing on the necessity of self-criticism and the ethical pursuit of artistic excellence over financial gain.</meta>

# THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

If we acknowledge that everyone is capable of judging the works of nature, we must also admit that everyone can see our mistakes. For the artist often errs in his work, and if you do not discover these errors within yourself, look at the work of others and you will profit from their mistakes.

Be curious, therefore, and patient in listening to the opinions of others: consider and examine well whether the critic is right or wrong to criticize; if he is right, correct your work; if not, act as if you had not heard him, and if he is a person you respect, demonstrate to him through reasoning why he is mistaken.

What true master would claim to observe by himself all the forms and effects of nature?

Such a man would seem to me a model of ignorance, for these forms and effects are of such diversity that our memory does not have the capacity to retain them.

Therefore, painter, take care that the greed for profit does not outweigh the honor of art: the preservation of this honor is more important than the prestige of riches. In this spirit, and for several other reasons, apply yourself to providing—through the drawing of the demonstrative form <note>The "demonstrative form" likely refers to a clear, structural representation that reveals the underlying mechanics or truth of a subject.</note>—the invention that your

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to embrace external criticism and prioritize the integrity and "honor" of their art over the pursuit of wealth. He emphasizes that nature is too vast for any single artist to master through personal observation alone.</summary>
<keywords>artistic criticism, ethics of art, observation of nature, artistic honor, memory, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

Then, ensure that in measurements and proportions subjected to perspective, nothing enters the work without the counsel of reason and natural effects. This is the path to making yourself honored in your art.

->26 v.<- <note>Refers to folio 26 verso, the back side of the 26th leaf in the original manuscript.</note>

If you wish to remember the appearance of a face by heart, learn many types of features by heart. Noses are of ten varieties in profile; from the front, they have twelve.

When you have to create a face from memory, carry with you a notebook where such features are noted. When you have glanced at the model, you will look in your notes to see which nose resembles it. You will make a small mark there. # THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

for the benefit of your profession, games based on a good judgment of the eye, concerning the truth of the widths and lengths of objects.

### 27 r. <note>Refers to folio 27 recto, the front side of the 27th leaf in the original manuscript.</note>

During the winter evenings, you should gather the nudes <note>Studies or drawings of the naked human form.</note> that you made during the summer and choose the best limbs and put them into practice.

The following summer, you shall choose someone who has not been raised wearing a <term>pourpoint</term> <note>A tight-fitting, padded tunic or doublet common in the Renaissance; Leonardo warns it can permanently compress or deform the natural shape of the body.</note>, so that the body is not deformed; you will have them take pleasant and graceful poses, and you will correct the limbs according to those you studied during the winter evenings.

Apply yourself to taking the good parts of beautiful faces whose beauty is confirmed by public opinion, rather than those praised by your own judgment, for you might be mistaken by preferring faces that have a similarity to your own. If you are ugly, you will not choose beautiful faces: painting is very likely to resemble its author, so take beauties as I have said and commit them to memory.

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to refine their anatomical studies during winter and warns against the "subconscious bias" where an artist's work reflects their own physical appearance. He suggests using public consensus on beauty to avoid personal error.</summary>
<keywords>artistic practice, anatomy, winter studies, pourpoint, aesthetic bias, facial beauty, Renaissance training, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

### *Composition.*

When you know perspective and the limbs by heart, consider the movements of men who speak, argue, or fight, and those of the spectators; and note them briefly in a small notebook of tinted paper, which you should always have with you, so that you do not have to erase, but to preserve; you shall keep these notebooks as your authors and masters.

Draw slowly, seeing first which are the strongest light and the strongest shadow, and how they blend together.

Let the shadows and lights unite without lines or marks, in the manner of smoke <note>original: "en façon de fumée." This refers to Leonardo's famous technique of <term>sfumato</term>, where colors and tones blend so subtly that they appear like smoke without harsh outlines.</note>.

When you have trained your hand and your judgment, you will draw quickly.

The painter must be solitary when he is attentive to speculation <note>In this context, "speculation" refers to deep observation and mental reflection on the principles of nature.</note>. If you are alone, you will be entirely your own; if you are accompanied, you will be only half your own.

in the works of others than in our own. By correcting the small faults of another, you will ignore your own, which are great.

To ward off this blindness, first establish a good understanding of perspective, then take note of the measurements of man and animals; observe as an architect the form of buildings, and remember that everything on the earth presents an infinite variety of forms.

The more knowledge you possess, the more your work will be praiseworthy, especially if you have followed and not rejected the lesson of nature.

When you paint, you should hold a flat mirror and look at your work in it often: there you will judge your errors; it is also good to stand up frequently; by interrupting yourself and returning to things, you bring to them a better judgment.

Young children have slender <term>joints</term> <note>original: jointures; here referring to the visible points of articulation</note> and the space between them is thick. The most praiseworthy figure is the one that best expresses, through action, the passion of its character. <note>Leonardo frequently emphasized that "motions of the mind"—emotions and thoughts—must be reflected in the "motions of the body."</note>

To achieve elegance, make the limbs extended, without too many muscles. <note>Leonardo often contrasted "grace" with the "force" seen in heavily muscled figures like those of Michelangelo.</note>

# THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

...you shall use this mixture in this way: the dust, which is a terrestrial and heavy thing, although by its lightness it rises easily and mingles with the air, does not willingly fall back down; its highest elevation is achieved by its lightest part, that which is least visible and almost blends with the color of the air. The smoke that mingles with the air becomes charged with dust, and the more it rises to a certain height, the more it appears as a dark cloud; the smoke reaches the top faster than the dust.

The smoke will take on a slightly <term>azure</term> <note>A sky-blue or light blue color.</note> tint, and the dust will take the color of the earth itself. On the side from which the light comes, this mixture of air, smoke, and dust will appear brighter than on the opposite side. The combatants will be seen less clearly, and one will see fewer differences between their highlights and shadows, the more they are enveloped in this turbulent air.

You shall make the faces and the people glow red, along with the air and the <term>fusiliers</term> <note>Soldiers armed with muskets or early firearms.</note> and everything near them; and this redness diminishes as it moves away from its cause. And the figures that are between you and the light, being far—

...distant, will appear dark against a light background, and their legs will be all the less visible as they approach the ground, because, at the bottom, the dust is thicker and more dense.

If you depict horses running away from the fray, make small clouds of dust for them, separated from one another by the distance of a horse's leap; and let this small cloud be less visible the further it is from the horse, and in that case, let it be higher, more scattered, and smaller. Closer to the horse, let it be more distinct, smaller, and thicker.

The air shall be full of arrows of various kinds: some rising, some descending, and others in a flat line; and the shots from the <term>fusils</term> <note>Early firearms or muskets.</note> should be accompanied by some smoke directed toward their target.

And the figures in the foreground, you shall make them dusty, including the hair, the eyebrows, and other smooth parts capable of holding the dust.

You shall depict the victors running with their manes and other light ornaments fluttering in the wind; with their brows lowered and thrusting forward the opposite limbs—that is to say, if a man sends forward his right foot, let the <insert>left</insert> arm, being tired, fall back. <note>Leonardo is describing the natural "cross-lateral" movement of the human body during a run to maintain balance.</note>

# MANUSCRIPT C

### TREATISE ON SHADOW AND LIGHT

56 pages written from 1490 to 1491, a final draft of older notes.

*Of all Leonardo's notebooks, this is the most thankless to read; lacking any figures of aesthetic beauty and devoid of general ideas, it is a collection of optical demonstrations that are difficult to grasp. It would require a commentary that would demand too much labor from anyone other than a specialist.*

*One may be surprised, however, that no oculist or ophthalmologist—none of those who specialize in the eye—has deigned to read and comment on such an important monograph.*

*On June 18, 1491, Leonardo organized the festivities for the marriage of Ludovico Sforza <note>Ludovico "Il Moro" Sforza, Duke of Milan and Leonardo's primary patron.</note> to Beatrice d'Este; he resumed work on the equestrian statue and occupied himself with the stables of Galeazzo San Severino.*

Among bodies equal in size and distance, the most brightly illuminated will appear the largest and the closest.

I have found that the stars which are closer to the horizon appear larger in aspect than the others, because they see and are seen with a greater amount of the solar body <note>Leonardo is exploring an atmospheric or optical theory here, suggesting that the position relative to the sun's light affects perceived size.</note> than when they are above us: and because they see more of the sun, they possess more light.

### 6 r.

The air is mixed with a moisture similar to
that of the earth, the excess of which falls back
once every 24 hours, then rises again with the heat
of the sun, which sustains it as long as it remains in
our hemisphere. The departure of the sun
returning it to its weight <note>original: "pesanteur," referring here to the gravitational pull or heaviness of the water droplets</note>, it falls back: this is
the dew in summer, and the white frost in winter.

### 6 v.

Force always pushes toward the principle
of the place where it was born; and weight pushes
toward the place of its departure. The places of
birth of force are innumerable; the
place of weight is unique, it is the earth. <note>Leonardo distinguishes here between "force" (impetus or energy applied to an object) and "weight" (gravity), noting that while energy can come from anywhere, gravity always pulls toward the center of the world.</note>

### 7 r.

### 7 r.

The eye preserves the resemblance of luminous things better than shadowy ones: for the eye is dark in itself, and like within like does not create a distinction. <note>Leonardo suggests that because the pupil is dark, it cannot easily distinguish dark objects against a dark background, whereas light objects provide necessary contrast.</note> At night, other obscure things are not preserved or even recognized by the eye.

There are three shapes of shadow: if the object that casts the shadow is equal in size to the light source, the shadow will be shaped like a column and has no end; if the object is larger than the light, its shadow is like an inverted and opposing pyramid and its length is without end; but if the object is smaller than the light, the shadow is like a finite pyramid, as is shown in eclipses of the moon. <note>Leonardo is applying Euclidean geometry to the study of optics, specifically how the relative size of a light source (like the sun) and an object (like the earth or moon) determines the shape of the resulting shadow (the umbra).</note>

### 7 v.

### II r.

The shape of the derivative shadow <note>original: "l'ombre dérivative" — the secondary shadow or penumbra cast through space</note> will always be in conformity with the shape of the original shadow <note>original: "l'ombre originale" — the primary shadow on the object itself</note>.
It is possible for a derivative shadow to form without an original shadow. <note>Leonardo likely refers to shadows cast by light passing through an aperture rather than being blocked by a solid body.</note>

### II v.

The same.

### 12 r.

Among things that are equal in size, whiteness, background, and length, that which is flatter will appear larger.
Iron, when half-reddened by heat, proves this, for the glowing part appears thicker than the other. <note>Leonardo observes the optical illusion where irradiation or glowing light makes an object appear to expand in size.</note>

### 12 v.

The variety of colors at a long distance is only recognizable in the parts struck by solar rays.

It is possible that mixed derivative shadows <note>original: "ombres mêlées dérivatives" — complex shadows formed by multiple light sources or reflections</note>, caused by a single light source falling upon various bodies, can intersect and overlap one another.

### 13 v.

It is impossible for simple derivative shadows, originating from various bodies and caused by a single light source, to ever fail to join or touch <note>Leonardo is exploring the geometric necessity of shadows converging when they originate from a single point of light.</note>.

### 14 r.

If many shaded bodies placed very close together, almost touching, are seen against a luminous field at a great distance, they will appear to be separated by a large interval. <note>This describes an optical illusion where distance increases the perceived gap between dark objects against a bright background.</note>

### 14 v.

Darkness: the absence of light.
Shadow: the diminution of light.
Primitive shadow <note>original: "Ombre primitive"</note>: that which is attached to the shaded bodies themselves.
Derivative shadow: that which detaches itself from the said bodies <note>The "primitive" shadow is what we now call the "core shadow" on the object, while the "derivative" is the "cast shadow" on the floor or wall.</note>.

**Reflected shadow**: that which is surrounded by an illuminated wall <note>original: "Ombre répercutée" — a shadow that receives secondary light bouncing off a nearby surface, making it less dark than a simple shadow.</note>.
**Simple shadow**: that which sees no part of the light source that causes it; it begins, therefore, at the extreme edge of the luminous bodies.

->15 r.<-

A rod or a knife moved with speed will appear double. A knife stuck into wood and pulled forcefully to one side vibrates and oscillates, like the string of a lute; this optical doubling occurs because the movement of the tip is very rapid. But tell me, why does a false lute string <note>A "false" string refers to one of uneven thickness or quality, which produces discordant sounds and irregular vibrations.</note> produce two or three images while vibrating, and sometimes four?

### 16 r.

If the eye accustomed to darkness suddenly sees the light, it closes. The <term>pupil</term> is pained by the too great quantity of light, and it contracts completely.

Reflections <note>original: "ressemblances" — Leonardo used this term to refer to the visual "images" or "species" that objects transmit through space to the eye or a mirror.</note> fall from the object to the mirror, and from the mirror to the eye, at equal angles; likewise, the voice enters the ear.

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's focus on optical effects and the perception of objects against backgrounds, Leonardo shifts his attention to hydraulic principles, the physical behavior of water, and the mechanics of force.</meta>

# THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

### 24 v.

That large stones can be rolled by water.

### 25 r.

If you walk in still water <note>original: "eau morte," literally "dead water," referring to stagnant or non-flowing water</note>, with every step, you push it toward the bank you are heading toward. Once you have left the water, you will see that it returns to its place.

### 25 v.

Ten strikes of one pound make ten pounds of striking force, yet they do not equal a single strike of ten pounds. <note>Leonardo is observing a principle of physics: a single large mass creates an impact force that many smaller masses cannot replicate, even if their total weight is the same.</note>

### 26 r.

How a stone at the bottom of a canal can damage it. <note>Leonardo often studied how obstructions in a watercourse create turbulence or "eddies" that can erode the canal's structure.</note>

### 26 v.

Water is the second of the four elements <note>Referring to the classical elements: earth, water, air, and fire</note>, the second least heavy and the second in mobility. It finds no rest except by joining its marine element; or, when not disturbed by the winds, it rests with its surface equidistant to the <note>The sentence is incomplete, but Leonardo likely meant "equidistant to the center of the world," reflecting the contemporary understanding of the Earth's gravity and curvature.</note>

<summary>Leonardo records observations on the movement of water, the erosion of canals, and a principle of mechanical impact, concluding with a definition of water as one of the four classical elements.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, mechanics, classical elements, impact force, canal engineering, fluid dynamics</keywords>
