<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s observations on the physical properties of water and begins a technical instruction on the perspective of portraiture.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>
<sig>27 r.</sig>
<sig>27 v.</sig>

->center of the world.<- It increases the moisture <note>original: "humeur," referring to the medieval concept of bodily fluids or vital moisture</note> of all soft bodies; nothing within it preserves its original form. No object lighter than water can penetrate it without force; it rises through heat as a subtle vapor. Cold congeals it, and stillness corrupts it. It takes on every odor, color, and flavor, yet possesses none of its own; it penetrates all porous bodies. Against its fury, no human defense prevails. In its motion, it supports things heavier than itself. It leaps as high as the point from which it fell. In its rushing descent, it submerges even the lightest objects. Its course is sometimes at the surface, sometimes in the middle, and sometimes at the bottom. Shallow water gnaws at the banks, and more so if the water is high; its upper parts do not weigh upon its lower parts.

# ASH

## I

Written around 1492 and consisting of 26 pages, this is a fragment of the *Treatise on Painting* <note>original: "Traité de la Peinture"; Leonardo’s famous collection of notes regarding the rules and techniques of art</note>. It was stolen by Libal <note>Guglielmo Libri (1803–1869), a notorious book thief and mathematician who stole thousands of manuscript pages from French public libraries</note> from Manuscript A at the Institute <note>The Institut de France in Paris</note>. It was sold to the same Lord Ashburnham, just as the Ashburnham I manuscript stolen from Manuscript B was.

### 4 r.

F. — Church of San Sepulcro <note>The Church of the Holy Sepulchre</note> in Milan. This building is inhabited <note>occupied or functional</note> both below and above, like the Holy Sepulchre <note>referring to the original tomb of Christ in Jerusalem</note>; and it is similar above and below, except that the top has the dome *cd* and the bottom has the dome *ab*. You descend 10 steps to enter the lower church, and you ascend 20 steps to enter the upper one: this is the space that exists between the floors of one church and the other.

## MANUSCRIPT A

### OF 126 PAGES (21-14) DATED 1492

*Leonardo was occupied with the <term>tiburio</term> <note>The lantern or crossing tower of a cathedral; specifically the octagonal structure over the center of the Milan Cathedral.</note> of the Milan Cathedral, the decoration of the Sforza Palace, the <term>Cenacolo</term> <note>The Last Supper.</note> and the equestrian statue of Francesco Sforza: his academy may have already been in existence for eight years.*

*This manuscript, the fourth book of the Arconati donation, the third of Bishop Bonsignori and A. de Venturi, is a <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where the sheets are folded twice to create four leaves.</note> described thus by Ravaisson: "it is composed of notebooks assembled in 16 leaves and bound in white <term>vellum</term> <note>A fine parchment made from animal skin.</note>, in the form of a portfolio, with a small clasp also in white <term>vellum</term>. On the cover, an 'A' written by Venturi and repeated inside on a blank page attached to the cover. According to the donation of 1637, this manuscript had 144 leaves; 51 are missing, and leaf 54 has been torn out. The pagination is in the hand of the master."*

*In 1490 Leonardo was still occupied with the <term>tiburio</term>, and he directed the decoration of several rooms at the Sforza Palace.*

### I r.

Recipe for preparing panels for painting.

**OPTICS.** — The sun, entering the house through window *a b*, enlarges the window and shrinks the shadow of the man; so that when he brings this <term>penumbra</term> <note>The partially shaded outer region of a shadow cast by an opaque object.</note> closer to the shadow which carries the real dimension of the opening, he will see the shadows as faded and blurred, by the power of the light opposing the passage of the solar rays...

[Diffuse light reduces the shadow of the man placed before the window].

To reproduce a nude, from nature or otherwise, hold a <term>plumb line</term> <note>A string with a weight at the bottom used to find a perfectly vertical line; Leonardo used this to ensure correct posture and alignment in his drawings.</note> in your hand to capture the intersection of the lines.

Measurement and division of the statue.

Divide the head into 12 parts, and each part into degrees, and each degree into 12 points, and each point into 12 minutes, and each minute into seconds, and each second into half-seconds.

<sig>2 r.</sig>

Every movement is either natural, or violent, or natural-violent—that is to say, mixed—as is seen in the pan of a balance scale as it descends. <note>Leonardo uses "natural" to mean movement toward a center of gravity and "violent" to mean movement caused by an external force.</note>

*Method of representing a relief at night.* — Place a paper that is not too transparent between the object and the light, and you will have a good silhouette.

*Glass pane.* — Perspective is nothing other than seeing an object located behind a smooth and transparent glass, upon which everything behind the glass is reflected; everything reaches the point of the eye through various pyramids, and these pyramids intersect on the glass. <note>The "pyramid of vision" is a core concept in Renaissance optics, where light rays form a triangle or pyramid with the eye as the apex.</note>

### Optical Figures.

The amplification of the luminous body <term>luminous body: an object that emits light</term> comes from the air through which it passes.

The shadowy body, seen along the line of incidence, will show no protruding part to the eye. Example: (*demonstrative figure*).

...there is no object so small that it is not larger than the retina where these <term>pyramids<note>Leonardo uses the term "pyramid" to describe the cone of light rays that travel from the edges of an object and converge at the eye.</note></term> culminate; therefore, if you take the lines from the edges of any body and continue them to a single point, they will take on a pyramidal shape <note>The original manuscript contains several revisions of this definition.</note>.

Perspective is a demonstrative reason <note>In this context, a "demonstrative reason" refers to a logical or mathematical proof based on observation.</note> through which experience confirms that every object sends its own likeness to the eye along pyramidal lines.

And bodies of equal size will form a larger or smaller angle at the tip of their pyramid, depending on the distance existing between them.

The point, indivisible due to its smallness, is the place where all the tips of the pyramids converge.

Separate forces do not all act at the same time or for the same purpose.

# THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

On supports. — A large number of small supports, when joined together, will sustain a greater weight than each taken separately.

*On lights.* — The union of small luminous bodies will increase the power of each of them. Place small lamps <note>The original French uses "lumignons," referring to small candles, wicks, or oil lamps.</note> along a straight line and stand in front of them at a certain distance; if you observe the quality of the light produced by these lamps and then bring them together, you will see that the spot where you were standing is better illuminated than before.

We know that the stars have a brightness similar to that of the moon; united, they would provide more light than the moon, but since they are scattered, even with a clear and starry sky, our part of the world remains dark if the moon is not in our hemisphere.

*Supports.* — Two separate columns that can each carry 100 pounds will, when joined together, support 300. <note>Leonardo frequently explored how the collective strength of materials often exceeds the sum of their individual parts due to increased stability.</note>

*On the pressure of weight.* — A support being loaded upright with a weight equidistant from its center can neither twist nor break, but only be driven downward; however, if the weight bears more on one part, the support will bend toward the side most pressed by the load.

<summary>Leonardo explores the principle of collective strength, arguing that combined light sources or structural supports are more effective than the sum of their individual parts. He also describes the physics of vertical loading, noting that an off-center weight causes a support to buckle rather than just sink.</summary>
<keywords>structural physics, additive light, optics, columns, weight distribution, astronomy, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

### 4 v.

The gathering of small heavy bodies produces a greater weight than that which they each have separately: weigh some lead shavings or crushed glass, then melt them, and you will see that the weight has increased. <note>Leonardo is observing a phenomenon that contradicts modern conservation of mass; he likely observed an increase in weight due to oxidation during the melting process or the removal of air trapped between the shavings.</note>

*On blows struck upon a sheet of lead.*

*On flight.* <note>Original: "De la fuite." In this context, Leonardo refers to the trajectory or "escape" of a projectile through the air.</note> — If a one-pound crossbow shoots an arrow one hundred fathoms <note>Original: "brasses," a traditional unit of measurement based on the reach of a man's arms, roughly equivalent to six feet.</note>, a ten-pound crossbow will not shoot its arrow one thousand fathoms. This is the case whenever a violent movement engenders a second one, the latter being performed by the object that the instrument pushes. <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that mechanical efficiency and range do not scale linearly due to factors like air resistance and the mechanics of the machine itself.</note>

*On flight.* — A stone thrown with great force will change the line of its course halfway through. <note>This likely refers to the point where the initial impetus begins to fail and gravity significantly alters the stone’s path into a curve.</note>

Other theorems on the blows of a hammer.

### 4 v.

More on the blow struck upon a lead rod with a hammer of a specific weight.

Figure of a screw with a lever. <note>This refers to a diagram in the original manuscript illustrating a mechanical advantage system.</note>

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of weight and ballistics, noting that the range of a projectile does not increase proportionally with the strength of the machine. He also records observations on how the path of a thrown object changes mid-flight and the impact of hammers on metal.</summary>

### 5 r.

Flight <note>Original: "La fuite." In this context, Leonardo refers to the trajectory or the "escape" of a projectile from its source.</note> is caused by motion, motion is born from force, and force from weight and motion combined.

*On weight (three theorems on the balance).* — If a beam carries one thousand pounds, how much will four beams placed one on top of the other carry? <note>Leonardo is investigating structural integrity and whether stacking materials increases their load-bearing capacity linearly or exponentially.</note>

### 5 v.

If you want to find the root of any number by means of geometry, proceed as follows:

---

To square <note>To "square" (carrer) in this sense means to calculate the area of a surface.</note> the surface of a body with flat surfaces, you must square the triangles and the task will be complete. <note>Leonardo refers to the method of triangulation, where any complex polygon can be broken down into triangles to determine its total area.</note>

### 6 r.

If you wish to measure a height using the shadow of the sun, take a rod of one fathom <note>Original: "brasse," a traditional unit of measurement based on the reach of a man's arms, roughly equivalent to six feet.</note>, plant it, and wait until the sun doubles its shadow; immediately measure the shadow of the tower, and if it is 100 fathoms, the tower will be 50; this is a good rule. <note>This is a practical application of the properties of similar triangles. If the shadow is twice the length of the rod, the tower's shadow will likewise be twice the height of the tower.</note>

*On the diminution of objects at various distances.* — Perspective. A small object seen from nearby, and a large object seen from afar, will appear equal in size when viewed under an equal angle.

*Perspective.* — I ask how much distance the eye can cover to see a non-luminous body such as a mountain: it will be seen better if the sun is beyond it, and its apparent distance depends on the position of the sun in the sky.

*Perspective of motion.* — Two things moving at the same speed, but at different distances, will not appear similar to you; and as many times as the first distance is contained within the second, so much slower will the second object appear to you than the first.

<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

caused by objects and ending at the eye, must necessarily occur along straight lines.

Just as a stone, thrown into water, becomes the center and cause of various circles, and just as sound spreads through the air in circular waves, so every body placed within the luminous air spreads itself in a circular manner and projects countless reflections into the surrounding area, appearing "all in all, and all in the smallest part." <note>Leonardo is describing the theory of "species," an early belief that objects radiate their entire form into the surrounding space, allowing the eye to capture the whole image from any single point.</note>

->10 r.<-

Perspective is that demonstrative method where experience affirms that every object sends its reflection to the eye via <term>pyramidal lines</term> <note>converging lines of sight that form a geometric pyramid, with the object as the base and the eye as the peak (apex)</note>; that is to say, by the lines which originate from the surface boundaries of objects and end at a single point. This point is located within the eye, the universal judge of all bodies. Because this point is so small that it can be described as indivisible, no object will be perceived by the eye unless it is larger than this point. It is therefore necessary that the lines originating from the object end in pyramids.

### 15 r.
<note>The "r" stands for recto, the front side of a leaf.</note>

The area of any circle contains 4 circles equal to one another.
<note>Leonardo is likely exploring the relationship between the diameter and the area; doubling the diameter of a circle quadruples its area.</note>

The area of any square contains 4 equal squares.

### 15 v.
<note>The "v" stands for verso, the back side of a leaf.</note>

Where there is the most contact, there is the greatest resistance, and the screw with square teeth travels through more space than the one with triangular teeth; it is true that the screw with triangular teeth yields more easily, having less contact and therefore more resistance.
<note>Leonardo is analyzing the friction and mechanical advantage of different screw thread profiles. "Resistance" here refers to the frictional force generated by the surface area of the threads.</note>

### On Cold.

Cold has two causes: the absence of heat and the movement of air. By itself, air is cold and dry, and if it is deprived of vapors, it readily assimilates the nature and resemblance of the things it touches; thus, in passing over a fragrant thing, such as musk or sulfur, it immediately becomes impregnated with it; and furthermore, if a luminous body is placed in the air, all the surrounding air will become bright. <note>Leonardo views air as a neutral medium that adopts the qualities—smell, light, or temperature—of the objects it encounters.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains how white surfaces adopt the colors of nearby objects and describes air as a neutral medium that takes on the properties of heat, light, and scent from its surroundings.</summary>
<keywords>optics, reflection, color theory, atmospheric physics, properties of air, heat and cold</keywords>

### Against perpetual motion.

No inorganic thing can move by itself; if it moves, it will be the effect of an unequal force, whether in its time, its motion, or its weight; and the rotation of the primary motor <note>original: "premier moteur." In Aristotelian physics, this refers to the initial source of motion that sets others in sequence.</note> having ceased, the second will immediately cease. <note>Leonardo argues here against the possibility of a "perpetual motion machine," asserting that energy cannot be created from nothing and that friction or the loss of the initial force will always bring a mechanical system to a halt.</note>

<summary>Leonardo proposes a theory that concentrated airflow creates extreme cold and asserts the physical impossibility of perpetual motion, arguing that all inorganic movement requires an external force.</summary>
<keywords>thermodynamics, concave mirrors, bellows, geodesy, astronomy, perpetual motion, mechanics, primary motor, physics</keywords>

### How the painter must position himself in relation 
### to the light and his subject. <note>original: "avec son relief." Here, "relief" refers to the three-dimensional quality of the model or object the artist is painting.</note>

In whatever position the painter stands, it will always be well, provided that his eye is between the shaded part and the luminous part of the body he intends to reproduce.

A broad, high, and moderate light is the one that best brings out the different parts of bodies.

*On the error committed in the judgment of limbs.* — The painter who has coarse hands will make them so in his paintings; and this will happen for every other limb unless constant study is vigilant. Consider then, O painter, what is ugly in your own person, and through study, take care to guard yourself against it. For if you are brutish <note>original: "bestial." This suggests a lack of refinement or intellectual clarity.</note> and without wit, your figures will resemble you, and similarly, whatever good or bad qualities you have within you will be found in your figures. <note>Leonardo is describing a psychological phenomenon he often noted: that artists tend to inadvertently paint their own physical likeness and temperament into their subjects.</note>

->26 r.<-

Water, by its weight, is the second element surrounding the earth; and any part of this water that finds itself outside of its natural sphere will strive to return to it. The higher it rises above its position as a circular element, the faster it will descend back down. Water seeks the low places, which it reaches when there is no obstacle; or else it rises as vapors and mist and falls back as rain, because the tiny particles of the mist gather together and form drops. Depending on the differences in altitude, various effects are produced, namely: water, snow, or hail. Water is buffeted by the movement of the air; it clings to it in bodies where cold has the greatest influence; it easily absorbs odors and flavors.

->27 r.<-

As soon as the air is illuminated, it becomes populated by countless images generated by various bodies and colors—images for which the eye acts as the target and the magnet. <note>Leonardo frequently used the metaphor of a magnet to describe how the eye attracts the "species" or visual forms of objects through the air.</note>

->(On movement)<-

->28 r<-

*Ballistics.* — If a cannonball strikes a round tower, the air situated on the opposite side resists, and the tower bursts open through its sides. <note>Leonardo explores early physics here, suggesting that the "resistance" of the air or the displacement of force causes a structural failure on the flanks of a curved building rather than just at the point of impact.</note>

If you are in a boat without any point of support outside of it, you will not be able to make it move. Similarly, if you are all huddled up inside a sack, you will be unable to change your location; however, if you pull one foot out of the sack and use it to push against the ground, you will move forward. <note>This is a remarkably clear early description of what would later be known as Newton's Third Law of Motion: to move a system, one must exert force against an external object.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explores the "persistence of vision" through the image of a spinning ember and describes the mechanics of force, noting that internal effort cannot produce movement without an external point of resistance.</summary>
<keywords>persistence of vision, optics, ballistics, mechanics, laws of motion, physics of impact, sensory perception</keywords>

This is what the flame does when it tries to expand inside the cannon <note>original: "bombarde," an early type of heavy artillery</note>: when this flame strikes and pushes against the resistant air, it causes the cannon to recoil; because that part of the flame which strikes the air cannot pass through it instantly, it exerts force in the opposite direction. <note>Leonardo is describing the principle of recoil—the "equal and opposite reaction"—long before Isaac Newton formally defined the laws of motion.</note>

<margin>Tiny human figures in the margin, demonstrating the center of gravity in man.</margin>

A person who is seated cannot stand up without the help of the arms, unless the part [of the body] in front of the pivot point weighs more than the part behind said point. <note>Leonardo observes that to rise from a chair, one must lean forward to shift their center of gravity over their feet, otherwise the weight of the torso keeps them pinned to the seat.</note>

One who climbs must shift his weight forward of his highest foot; thus, a person will always carry their weight in the direction of their movement.

One who runs leans toward his goal, and therefore places his foot forward. One who descends a slope keeps his balance on his heels, whereas one who climbs it while running keeps it on the balls of the feet. One who runs on flat ground lands first on the heels and then on the balls of the feet.

When you draw nude figures, do so in their entirety,

### Why is a projectile shot further by a bow than by a long crossbow?

->30 r.<-

When the mounted crossbowman <note>original: *arbalétrier*; a soldier specialized in using the crossbow, a weapon Leonardo frequently sought to improve</note> draws his weapon, he spurs the horse, quickly thrusts his chest forward, and extends his arms with the crossbow. Four movements are thus combined to propel an arrow: the movement of the horse, the movement of the chest, the movement of the arms throwing themselves forward from the chest, and the movement of the string.

The same amount of force you apply to bend your crossbow is the same amount it will possess upon release.

->30 v.<-

### On the strength of man.

A man pulling a weight that is in equilibrium with him can only pull according to his own weight.

The greatest force a man can exert is obtained by placing his feet on a scale platform and bracing his shoulders against a wall; he will then lift as much as his own weight <note>Leonardo is observing that without external leverage, a human's physical output is limited by the counter-resistance of their own mass</note>.

### 34 r.

->There is no force without movement, nor movement without force.<-

### 34 v.

Force.

I say that force is a violence, a spiritual virtue <note>Leonardo uses "spiritual" in the classical and medieval sense of "incorporeal" or "immaterial"—a power that lacks physical substance but produces physical effects.</note>, an invisible power, which, through an accidental, external violence, is generated by movement, introduced and infused into bodies that it diverts from their natural state of being, endowing them with a marvelously powerful activity. <note>In the physics of Leonardo's time, "accidental" refers to a property that is not essential to an object's nature; force is seen as a temporary state imposed upon an object from the outside.</note> It compels every created thing to change shape and position, rushes with fury toward its own extinction, and proceeds, diversifying itself according to its causes. Slowness increases it and speed exhausts it; it is born through violence and dies from its freedom, and the greater it is, the faster it dies. It furiously repels whatever opposes its destruction; it can conquer and kill the cause that obstructs it, and once victorious, it dies. Its power increases through obstacles; it furiously overthrows everything that stands in the way of its death. Every thing seeks to endure; every thing constrained constrains in turn. Nothing... <note>The sentence ends abruptly here, presumably continuing on the following page.</note>

<vocab>force, movement, spiritual virtue, Leonardo da Vinci, dynamics, physics</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo provides a poetic and philosophical definition of "force" as an invisible, immaterial power that disrupts the natural state of objects and seeks its own exhaustion through action. He explores the paradoxical relationship between force, resistance, and movement.</summary>
<keywords>force, dynamics, spiritual virtue, movement, physics, resistance, kinetic energy, Aristotelian physics</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 138: "Nothing moves without force." It continues Leonardo's exploration of the relationship between force, movement, and weight, specifically looking at how force manifests through expansion (combustion) and tension (elasticity).</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...moves without force. The body in which it is incarnated changes neither in volume nor in weight. None of its movements are lasting; it increases through effort and disappears in rest. The body it possesses no longer has freedom. Often it generates itself; from acquired force, a new force is born.

Force is born from movement; it is infused into weight; and likewise, the blow <note>The original French *le coup* refers to percussion or impact—the energy released when a moving object strikes another.</note> comes from movement infused into weight. Force causes movement, and movement causes force.

Force has three offices <note>Functions or duties.</note> which are subdivided infinitely: pulling, pushing, and immobilizing.

It manifests in two ways: by the sudden expansion of a rare body within a dense body (the multiplication of fire inside a bombard <note>An early type of large-caliber cannon or mortar.</note>). Thus water and wind drive away everything that opposes their path; or by condensation, as in bent and twisted bodies (the crossbow) which tend to straighten themselves and repel whatever opposed their course.

The blow is born from the death of movement, and movement is born from the death of force.

->35 r.<-

Violence is composed of four things:

<summary>Leonardo examines the interconnected cycle of force and movement, defining force's "offices" and how it is generated through either expansion—like gunpowder—or the tension of elastic objects like crossbows.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s explorations into the fundamental principles of mechanics, specifically the relationship between gravity and energy.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

weight, force, motion, and impact; the most powerful is that which has the shortest duration.

Weight is total in all its perpendicular opposition. <note>Leonardo refers here to "opposition" as the resistance or surface directly beneath a falling object.</note>

Oblique <note>slanted or angled</note> opposition will not offer resistance to the descent of the weight.

Every weight desires to descend toward the center <note>the center of the Earth</note> by the shortest path: the less oblique the opposition, the greater the resistance. Necessity attracts it and opulence <note>abundance of energy</note> drives it away. In its function of compressing and weighing down, it resembles force. Weight is conquered by force, just as force is conquered by weight. One can see weight without force, but one does not see force without weight. Weight desires stability, force is always active; weight does not grow weary, while force struggles incessantly. The more a weight falls, the more it increases; the more force falls, the more it diminishes. Weight is natural and eternal, force is morbid <note>original: "morbide"; meaning temporary or resulting from an external disturbance</note> and accidental; weight desires to endure, while force aspires to end.

->35 v.<-

Weight is of lesser power than the three other passions that are within it: force, motion, and impact. Force, more powerful than weight, is less durable; motion is born from force; impact is the son of motion and the grandson of force, and all are born from weight. <note>Leonardo creates a genealogy of physics here, suggesting that gravity (weight) is the ultimate ancestor of all mechanical action.</note>

### *Theorems of gravity.*

The more force extends, from wheel to wheel, from lever to lever, from screw to screw, the more it is simultaneously powerful and slow. <note>This describes the principle of mechanical advantage: gaining power at the expense of speed.</note>

->36 r.<-

*Perspective.* — All things send their image to the eye by way of pyramids; the closer these are intersected to the eye, the smaller the image of their reality will be. <note>Leonardo’s "visual pyramid" is a core concept of Renaissance perspective: he imagined lines of sight as a pyramid with the object as the base and the eye as the apex.</note> You shall therefore intersect the pyramid with a plane <note>original: "paroi"; literally a wall or partition, here referring to the surface of the painting</note> that touches the base of the pyramid.

->37 r. and v.<-

->*Perspective Theorem.*<-

->38 r.<-

The object closest to the eye always appears larger than an identical object that is further away.

->38 v.<-

Perspective is of such a nature that it makes what is flat appear in relief, and what is in relief appear flat. <note>This captures the essence of *chiaroscuro* and foreshortening—using geometry and light to trick the eye into seeing depth on a two-dimensional surface.</note>

### 40 v.

If you wish to represent an object from a close distance so that it produces the same effect as natural things, you should create a window the size of your face, or perhaps a hole through which you would look. In this way, your work—having a proper distribution of light and shadow—will produce a natural effect. Otherwise, do not concern yourself with representing anything at all, unless you take your point of view from a distance twenty times the greatest width and height of the object you are painting. <note>Leonardo is discussing the "painter's window," a device used to maintain consistent perspective and avoid the distortion that occurs when viewing a subject too closely.</note>

->*A demonstration follows.*<-

->*Theorems of perspective.*<-

### 43 r.

->*Regarding statues. — Explanatory figure of the*<-
->*aforementioned box.*<-

If you wish to make a figure of marble, first make it in clay; once it is finished and dried, place it in a box large enough—after the clay is removed—to receive the block of marble in which you wish to reveal a figure similar to the one in clay. After placing the clay figure in the box, take some rods... <note>Leonardo is describing a "pointing" system, a mechanical method using rods or measurements to accurately carve a marble block based on a preliminary model.</note>

<meta>The text breaks mid-sentence as the description of the measuring rods continues on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Leonardo provides instructions on maintaining realistic perspective by viewing subjects through a fixed aperture and outlines a mechanical method for translating a clay model into a marble statue using a measuring box.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, optics, sculpture technique, marble carving, clay modeling, painter's window, Renaissance engineering</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the "pointing" box for sculpture and transitions into observations on the physics of motion and ballistics.</meta>

<header>THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...rods that fit exactly through its holes, and push them until they touch the figure at various points; tint the part of these rods that protrudes from the box with black, and make a mark on both the rod and its hole. Then, remove the clay figure from the box, replace it with your block of marble, and carve away enough of the marble so that all the rods disappear into their holes up to the marks. And to do this better, let the box be removable while leaving its base under the marble; in this way, you can easily lift parts of it with iron tools. <note>This "pointing" technique allowed a sculptor to mechanically transfer the exact proportions of a model to stone, ensuring the finished work matched the original design.</note>

->43 v.<-

The midpoint of the direct path made by a heavy body, when traveling violently through the air, possesses more power and impact upon an obstacle than at any other part of its course. <note>Leonardo is exploring the concept of "impetus" or momentum, observing that a projectile's force is not necessarily greatest at the moment of release or at the end of its flight.</note>

<header>THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...of power, which strikes the object between two equal angles. <note>Leonardo is describing a perpendicular impact. When a projectile strikes a surface at a right angle (90 degrees), the angles on either side of the path are equal, resulting in the maximum transfer of force.</note>

The blow struck against an object between unequal angles is weak, and the rebound <note>original: *ressaut*</note> following the impact will result in two different movements. <note>In a glancing blow, the force is dissipated, and the projectile deflects away from the point of impact rather than transferring all its energy into the target.</note>

The detonation of the bombard <term>bombard|a primitive, large-caliber cannon used to hurl heavy stone balls</term> comes from the impact of the flame against the air; and the louder the bombard's explosion, the better it projects the cannonball, like a man pressing his back against a wall and pushing with his hands; he exerts force with his spine against the wall, because the arm, as it stiffens, exerts force both within the man and upon the object. <note>Leonardo uses a biomechanical analogy to explain the concept of recoil and the "equal and opposite reaction" that occurs when a cannon is fired.</note>

Every support offers more resistance to the weight placed upon it as its parts are more unified. <note>Leonardo is observing that the structural strength of a pillar or column is dependent on the cohesion of its material; a solid or "unified" structure can bear more weight than one made of loose or fragmented parts.</note>

### 52 v.

The crash of a bombard <note>A "bombard" was an early type of large-caliber cannon used in siege warfare.</note> fired over the water will kill all the animals that are within that water.

On noise.

If you take a small resonant vessel and cover it with wet calfskin parchment, and once it is dry, if you insert a small waxed cord and pull it back through with a glove waxed with the substance used to seal beehives <note>likely propolis or beeswax</note>, you will hear a strange noise.

### 53 r.

A beam that is longer than 10 or 20 times its thickness will be lacking in strength.

Remember never to make openings <note>such as doors or niches</note> beneath the spaces between windows. <note>Leonardo warns against this because the "piers" or wall sections between windows carry the vertical load; hollowing out the space beneath them compromises the building's structural integrity.</note>

### 53 v.

On the quality of beams.

I say that the movement made by a falling weight is equivalent to the movement made by force.

### 55 r.

### 55 v.

Man is called a **microcosm** <note>original: "microcosme"</note> (small world) by the ancients, and the epithet is just. If man is composed of earth, water, air, and fire, so too is the world; if man has a skeletal system beneath the framework of flesh, the world has its rocks which support the earth; if man has a lake of blood where the lungs expand and contract in breathing, the world has its ocean, which also rises and falls every six hours with the breathing of the universe; if from this lake of blood derive the veins which branch throughout the human body, the ocean traverses the world in veins of water. The world lacks only the nervous system, which is intended for movement and is useless to things destined for a perpetual stability; but in all other points, man and the world are similar.

<meta>This page continues the comparison between the human body and the Earth, completing the analogy of the circulation of "humors" before moving into an explanation of meteorology and the formation of precipitation.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

the earth and the peaks are farther from the sea the higher they rise above its level. Therefore, if the world did not resemble man, how could seawater, which is so much lower than the mountains, rise to their summits by its own nature? It is probable that the same cause that holds the blood at the top of a man's head also holds water at the summits of the mountains.

*Of heat in the world and...* — Where there is life, there is heat, and where there is useful heat, there is the movement of humors <note>In Leonardo’s time, "humors" referred to the vital fluids of a body; here he applies the medical concept to the Earth's water cycle.</note>. And if heat moves moisture, cold congeals it. Furthermore, one sees the igneous element <note>The "element of fire," which Leonardo identifies with the sun’s heat and its power to draw moisture upward.</note> drawing up vapors, dark mists, and thick clouds that rise from the seas, lakes, rivers, and valleys. Drawn in this way, they rise to the cold region; the leading part stops, as heat and moisture cannot mix with cold and dryness. This stalled part is increased by the others and forms clouds; carried by the winds from one region to another, their density makes them heavy and they fall as rain. If the solar heat acts with greater intensity, the clouds drawn higher into the cold zone freeze there, and this produces hail.

The same <term>caloric</term> <note>A historical term for the substance of heat, which Leonardo believes acts as a lifting force</note>, which holds up such a weight of water as we see falling in rain, draws it from the base of the mountains and raises it to the <term>summit</term> <note>original: "cîme"</note> where the water finds some crevices, hollows them out further, and produces rivers.

### 56 r.

Water is like the blood that the <term>vital heat</term> <note>The internal warmth believed to animate living beings</note> causes to circulate in the head of man. At death, the cooled blood descends to the lower parts: similarly, when the sun warms the head of a man, the blood flows there in such quantity that it forces the veins and engenders headaches.

In this way, the veins branch out through the body of the earth, and the <term>natural heat</term> attracts the water and maintains it upon the <term>summits</term>.

The water that passes through a subterranean passage in the body of the mountain will be as if dead, and will not rise because it is not warmed by the <term>natural heat</term>.

Water will not go from one place to another unless the second is lower than the first. As for that part of the sea which you said, with a false imagination, was so high that it—

## 57 r.

If one attributes the <term>ebb and flow</term> <note>The tides (French: flux et reflux)</note> to the moon, the augmenter of cold, this seems impossible. *Concerning the center of the Ocean.* — The center of the <term>watery sphere</term> <note>Leonardo views the Earth as a series of concentric elemental spheres; the "watery sphere" refers to the global sea level forming a perfect geometric sphere.</note> is the true center of the roundness of our world. The center of the earth is located at a point equidistant from the level of the ocean, and not from the level of the land.

We see the Nile, after a course of 3,000 <note>Likely referring to miles.</note>, flow into the Mediterranean waters; we will find that the Nile is ten thousand <note>Leonardo does not specify the unit here, but is referring to a measure of vertical drop or elevation.</note> lower at its mouth than at its source. We see also the Rhine, the Rhône, and the Danube starting from Germanic regions, as if from the center of Europe, to take their course toward the seas: one to the east, another to the north, the last toward the southern seas; if you consider everything well, you will see that the plains of Europe form a mass higher than the highest peaks of the <term>maritime mountains</term> <note>Mountains located near the coast.</note>; now imagine how much higher their peaks themselves are than the sea shores.

What is the moon?

you see the sun reflected in the sea at its setting, it will appear larger by 10 miles, because its reflection on the water will occupy more than 10 nautical miles. If you were on the moon, the sun would seem to be reflected in as many seas as it illuminates during the day, and the earth would appear to you in that water like the dark spots that are in the moon, which, seen from the earth, creates the same effect for us as our world would for men who might inhabit the moon <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that the moon's "spots" are topographical features and suggests that Earth would look similar if viewed from space</note>.

When everything we see of the moon is illuminated, it sends all its light back to us; as the sun's rays bounce off its ocean, it casts less humidity upon us, and the less it shines, the more it harms <note>original: "plus elle luit, plus elle nuit." Leonardo is referencing a contemporary belief in the "cold" or "humid" influence of moonlight on the Earth's atmosphere</note>.

->64 v.<-

### Why it is that luminous bodies appear, seen from afar, larger than they are.

*Experiment.* — If you want to see the true size of luminous bodies, make a hole with a lace-tag <term>ferret d’aiguilette: the small metal tip at the end of a lace or cord</term> on a thin board, and bring it close to the eye; by moving the board quickly forward and backward, you will see the light increase and decrease.

<summary>Leonardo discusses the optical expansion of the sun's reflection on the sea and theorizes how Earth would appear from the moon, concluding with an experiment on the perceived size of light sources.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, optics, reflection, moon, sun, atmospheric effects, experiment, 64v</keywords>

->3 v. 4 r.<-

### Latin Conjugations.
<note>Leonardo often practiced Latin grammar in his notebooks to improve his scholarly standing.</note>

->5 r.<-

*Love of virtue* (1). — The calandra (the lark), if brought before a sick person who is destined to die, turns its back to them and never looks at them. If the patient is to recover, the bird never loses sight of them and is the cause of their illness departing.

In this way, the love of virtue turns away from anything vile and sad and contemplates honest and virtuous things. It has its homeland in the noble heavens, just like birds in the green forests upon flowering branches; and this love shows itself best in adversity, like a light that shines all the more when it is in the darkness.

---

(1) Although this symbolic bestiary <note>A descriptive or anecdotal treatise on animals, often used to teach moral lessons.</note> is for the most part drawn from a work published in Venice in 1474, it reproduces medieval ideas and could be found implemented in the sculptures of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries. Perhaps the *Natural Mirror* <note>original: "Speculum naturale"</note> by Vincent of Beauvais, reader to Saint Louis, would provide most of the bizarre and amusing traits that the Master has extracted from the *Flower of Virtue, which treats human vices and how one must acquire virtue* <note>original: "Fiore di vertu che tratta vitti humani, et comme si deve acquistare la virtie"</note>, reprinted in 1740, in Rome.

<summary>Leonardo uses the legend of the calandra lark to illustrate how virtue seeks out the noble and turns away from the vile, mirroring medieval moral allegories. The translator's note identifies Leonardo's likely sources in popular 15th-century moral treatises and encyclopedias.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, symbolic bestiary, calandra lark, virtue, Vincent de Beauvais, Natural Mirror, Flower of Virtue, moral allegory, medieval science</keywords>

->6 r.<-

*Peace.* — It is said that the beaver, when pursued, knowing that it is for the medicinal virtue <note>The "virtue" here refers to the perceived healing power of castoreum, a substance produced by beavers, which was highly valued in ancient and medieval medicine.</note> of its testicles and being unable to flee, stops; then, having made peace with the hunters, it tears off its testicles with its sharp teeth and leaves them to its enemies.

*Anger.* — It is said that the bear, when it goes to the hives to take the honey and the bees sting it, leaves the honey and, wanting to take revenge on all of them, it takes revenge on none; it becomes enraged and, rolling on the ground in exasperation, it agitates its paws in vain.

---

->7 v.<-

*Envy.* — It is said of the kite <note>A medium-sized bird of prey in the hawk family.</note> that when it sees its young in the nest are too fat, it pecks at their sides out of envy and leaves them without food.

*Joy.* — Joy is embodied by the cock <note>The rooster.</note> who rejoices at the slightest thing and sings with lively and joyful movements.

*Sadness.* — Sadness is compared to the raven, which, when it sees its children born all white, leaves them with the laments of a... <note>The text cuts off here; according to medieval lore, ravens believed white chicks were not their own and refused to feed them until their black feathers grew in.</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes a series of animal allegories—the beaver, bear, kite, cock, and raven—to illustrate human virtues and vices, drawing on traditional medieval bestiary lore. These entries reflect his interest in using nature as a mirror for human morality and psychology.</summary>

<keywords>bestiary, allegory, beaver, bear, kite, rooster, raven, moral philosophy, Leonardo da Vinci, medicinal virtue, envy, anger</keywords>

->6 v.<-

**Gratitude.** — Gratitude is characteristic of hoopoes <note>The hoopoe is a colorful bird found across Afro-Eurasia, distinguished by its "crown" of feathers.</note>. They recognize the blessing of life and the care they have received by building a nest for their elderly parents; they brood over them, feed them, pluck out their old and damaged feathers with their beaks, and, using certain herbs, they restore their life and return them to a state of health.

**Avarice.** — The toad feeds on earth and always remains thin because it never eats its fill; so great is its fear that the earth might eventually run out. <note>In medieval lore, the toad was a frequent symbol of greed or miserliness, specifically the fear of losing one's resources.</note>

->7 r.<-

**Ingratitude.** — Doves, as soon as they are old enough to feed themselves, begin to fight against their father, and this battle does not end until they have driven him away; they then take their mother as their mate.

*Correction.* — When the wolf prowls around a sheepfold and places its paw incorrectly so as to make a noise, it bites its own foot to punish itself for its clumsiness.

*Flattery.* — The siren sings so sweetly that she lulls the mariners <note>original: "nautoniers," an archaic term for sailors</note> to sleep; she then climbs onto the ships and kills the sleeping sailors.
*Prudence.* — The ant, by natural counsel, provides in summer for the winter by killing the harvested seeds so that they do not germinate; they then feed upon them at the appointed time.
*Folly.* — The wild ox hates the color red; hunters cover the trunk of a tree with red, the ox charges and drives its horns into it, and then the hunters kill it.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s bestiary, moving from the traits of the eagle, ant, and ox to the social virtues of justice, truth, and fidelity illustrated through the behaviors of bees, partridges, and cranes.</meta>

Justice. — It is represented by the king of the bees <note>Until the 17th century, the queen bee was commonly mistaken for a male monarch.</note> who orders and arranges everything with reason. Some go among the flowers, others work, others fight with the wasps, others remove the refuse, and others accompany the king and form his procession.

When he is old and without wings, they carry him, and if any among them fails in its duty, it is punished without mercy.

*Truth.* — Although partridges steal eggs from one another, nevertheless, the chicks born from these eggs always return to their true mothers.

*Fidelity.* — Cranes are so faithful to their king that at night, while he sleeps, some stand around the meadow to keep watch in the distance; others always stand holding a stone in one claw, so that if sleep should overcome them, the stone would fall and, by its noise, wake them. There are others who sleep around the king, and every night they take turns so that the monarch lacks for nothing.

<summary>This page concludes a section of Leonardo’s bestiary, using the organized society of bees, the instincts of partridges, and the watchful nature of cranes to personify Justice, Truth, and Fidelity.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Bestiary, Allegory, Justice, Truth, Fidelity, Bees, Partridges, Cranes, Virtue</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s "Bestiary," a collection of short fables where animal behaviors serve as allegories for human virtues and vices. This follows the tradition of medieval moralizing texts.</meta>

*Falsehood.* — The fox, when he sees a flock of birds or magpies, throws himself to the ground in such a way that, with his mouth open, he appears dead; the birds come to peck at his tongue and he snaps up their heads. <note>Leonardo draws here from the "Physiologus," an ancient text that attributed symbolic moral traits to animals.</note>

<sig>9 v.</sig>

*Deceit.* — The mole has very small eyes and always remains underground. It lives as long as it stays hidden, but as soon as it comes into the light it dies, because it has made itself known.
So it is with deceit. <note>original: "Mensonge" - can also be translated as "The Lie."</note>

*Valor.* — The lion is never afraid; he fights with a bold spirit as a fierce adversary against a multitude of hunters, always seeking to strike the first one who attacked him.

*Fear.* — The hare is always trembling, and the leaves falling from the trees in autumn keep him in perpetual terror and put him to flight.

<sig>10 r.</sig>

*Magnanimity.* — The falcon hunts only large birds; he would rather let himself die than <note>The text cuts off here and likely continues on the following page regarding the falcon's refusal to eat small prey.</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes the symbolic characteristics of the fox, mole, lion, hare, and falcon to illustrate human traits such as falsehood, valor, and fear. These entries function as a moralizing bestiary where animal nature reflects ethical truths.</summary>

than to feed on small prey or on foul-smelling flesh. <note>This completes the entry for "Magnanimity" from the previous page, where the falcon's noble nature prevents it from stooping to lesser prey.</note>

*Vainglory.* — It is said that the peacock is more subject to this than any other animal; it constantly contemplates the beauty of its tail, spreading it out in the shape of a wheel, and with its cry, it attracts the animals around it. This is the last vice that one can overcome.

->10 v.<-

*Constancy.* — The phoenix, awaiting its renewal by instinct, endures the searing flames that consume it for its rebirth. <note>The phoenix was a mythical bird believed to burn itself on a funeral pyre and rise young again from the ashes.</note>

*Inconstancy.* — The swift <note>original: "martinet"</note> is inconstant, always in motion so as not to endure the slightest discomfort.

*Temperance.* — The camel, the most lustful of animals, would travel a thousand miles to follow a female; but if it lived continually with its mother or its sisters, it would never touch them, so well does it know how to restrain itself. <note>Leonardo uses "temperance" here in the sense of self-restraint and moral moderation.</note>

*Intemperance.* — The unicorn <note>original: "licorne ou unicorne"</note> does not know how to overcome its longing for young maidens; it forgets its ferocity and wildness, and without suspicion, it goes to the seated maiden and falls asleep in her lap, and thus the hunters capture it.

<summary>Leonardo continues his allegorical bestiary, using the peacock, phoenix, swift, camel, and unicorn to illustrate the human virtues of constancy and temperance, and the vices of vainglory and intemperance.</summary>

<keywords>bestiary, allegory, peacock, phoenix, camel, unicorn, temperance, vainglory, moral philosophy, Leonardo da Vinci manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s moralizing bestiary, transitioning from the virtues of constancy and temperance to the qualities of humility, pride, abstinence, gluttony, and chastity, using animals as symbolic archetypes.</meta>

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

*Humility.* — The greatest example of this is seen in the lamb, which submits to every animal. When they are given as food to captive lions, they submit to them as if to their own mother, so much so that lions have been seen refusing to kill them.

->11 v.<-

*Pride.* — The falcon wishes to surpass all other birds of prey; it desires to be alone, and many times it has been seen attacking the eagle, the king of birds.

*Abstinence.* — The wild donkey, when it goes to the spring and finds the water clouded, no matter how great its thirst, it abstains from drinking until the water clears. <note>Leonardo presents this as a lesson in patience and self-denial.</note>

*Gluttony.* — The vulture is so voracious that it would travel a thousand miles to eat carrion; for this reason, it follows armies. <note>The vulture's association with battlefields was a common trope in Renaissance natural history.</note>

->12 r.<-

*Chastity.* — The turtledove is never unfaithful to its mate, and if one dies, the other observes a perpetual chastity; it never again perches on a green branch and never drinks clear water. <note>In medieval and Renaissance folklore, the mourning turtledove was said to drink only muddy water and avoid green trees to show its grief.</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes a series of moral allegories using animal behaviors, contrasting the humility of the lamb and the abstinence of the wild donkey with the pride of the falcon and the gluttony of the vulture.</summary>
<keywords>humility, pride, abstinence, gluttony, chastity, bestiary, Leonardo da Vinci, symbolism, lamb, falcon, vulture, turtledove</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s moralizing bestiary, moving from the virtues of chastity and abstinence on the previous page to the vices of lust and the virtues of moderation and renewal, using the bat, ermine, and eagle as symbolic figures.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

*Lust.* — The bat, with its unrestrained lust, observes no natural law; male with male, female with female, they practice copulation just as they meet, by chance. <note>Leonardo often used animals to illustrate human moral failings; here, the bat represents a lack of order and restraint.</note>

*Moderation.* — The ermine eats only once a day; it allows itself to be captured by hunters rather than stain its nobility <note>original: "gentillesse," referring here to the purity or "gentleness" of its white fur</note> in a muddy den while fleeing. <note>The ermine was a popular Renaissance symbol of purity and moderation, often associated with the motto "Death rather than dishonor."</note>

->12 V.<- <note>This refers to the "Verso" or back side of the 12th leaf of the original manuscript.</note>

*Eagle.* — When it is old, it flies so high that it burns its feathers: nature allows them to be renewed by falling into a little water. <note>This reflects a medieval myth of the eagle's rejuvenation, similar to the phoenix.</note>

If its young cannot withstand the sight of the sun, it does not feed them. Let no bird that values its life approach its nest. The animals fear it, but it does not harm them; it leaves them the remains of its hunt.

*Lumerpa.* — This creature is born in Great Asia and shines so brightly that it removes its own shadows. In dying, it preserves this radiance and its feathers never fall out; a feather that is detached no longer shines. <note>The "Lumerpa" appears to be a mythical bird, likely derived from Leonardo's readings of medieval encyclopedias or travelers' tales.</note>

<summary>Leonardo continues his symbolic bestiary, contrasting the bat's perceived lack of natural law with the ermine's legendary purity and the eagle's solar majesty and renewal.</summary>
<keywords>lust, moderation, renewal, bestiary, Leonardo da Vinci, bat, ermine, eagle, lumerpa, symbolism</keywords>

*Pelican.* — He bears a great love for his young; if he finds them killed in the nest by a serpent, he pierces his own heart before them and, bathing them with a rain of his blood, he brings them back to life. <note>The pelican was a common medieval symbol for Christ’s sacrifice and the resurrection.</note>

It has no digestive organs <note>original: "membres nourriciers," referring to organs that process food</note> and cares for no other food than the fire, where it constantly renews its skin <note>original: "écorce," literally meaning bark or shell, here used for the creature's outer layer</note>.

*Chameleon.* — It lives on air and is preyed upon by birds. To be safe, it flies above the clouds, and so high that no bird can follow it.
To this height, only one gifted for the heavens may go. <note>In the Renaissance, the chameleon was often thought to live on nothing but air, making it a symbol of the ethereal or the spiritual.</note>

**Stork.** — This bird heals itself by drinking salt water; if it finds its mate has been unfaithful, it abandons her.

*Bat.* — Representing vice, which cannot remain where virtue exists; the bat loses its sight where light is most brilliant, and even more so when it looks at the sun.

*Partridge.* — It transforms from female to male and forgets its former sex. Out of envy, it steals the eggs of others and broods them, but the chicks follow their true mother. <note>Medieval bestiaries often used the partridge's supposed egg-stealing as an allegory for the Devil attempting to steal souls belonging to God.</note>

*Swallow.* — With the Celandine <note>original: "Chélidoine," a plant whose name is derived from the Greek word for swallow, *chelidōn*</note> she restores sight to her blind young.

**Oyster, for treachery.** — It opens at the full moon, and the crab throws a stone into it <note>Leonardo likely refers to the fable where the crab prevents the oyster from closing its shell so it can be eaten; it serves as a warning against being too open or vulnerable to enemies.</note>

or a piece of straw, it cannot close itself and becomes the prey of the crab. So it is for the mouth that gives away a secret and makes itself the prey of whoever listens.

*Basilisk.* — The snakes flee from it; the weasel, by means of rue <note>A bitter medicinal herb</note>, fights with it and kills it (rue stands for virtue).

*The Asp.* — It carries sudden death in its fangs, and, so as not to hear the enchantments <note>Likely a reference to snake-charming</note>, it stops up its ears with its tail.

->15 r.<-

*Dragon.* — It binds the legs of the elephant and the latter falls upon it, and both die. (In dying, it takes its revenge).

*Viper.* — This creature, during its mating, opens its mouth and at the end clenches its teeth and kills the husband; then its young grow within its body, tear open its belly, and kill their mother.

*Scorpion.* — Saliva spat upon the scorpion, while fasting, kills it.

In the likeness of abstinence, gluttony takes away and kills the sick who are dependent on that gluttony.

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci uses a series of animal allegories involving the basilisk, asp, dragon, and viper to illustrate moral failings and the consequences of indiscretion and gluttony.</summary>

*Caterpillar.* — Of virtue in general.
The caterpillar, which weaves around itself with admirable skill and subtle labor its new dwelling, emerges from it with painted and beautiful wings, and by them rises toward the heavens.

*Spider.* — The spider brings forth the artistic and masterful web, which yields as its reward the promised prey.

Nothing is more to be feared than an evil reputation.

Fatigue flees with Fame in her arms, nearly hidden. <note>This likely refers to the idea that great achievements (Fame) are born from exhausting effort (Fatigue), which is often overlooked once the glory is won.</note>

river; they do not fight for females like other animals. They are so merciful that, by instinct, they do no harm to the weakest. If they encounter a flock of sheep, they move them aside with their trunk so as not to trample them. They never cause harm unless they are provoked. When one of them falls into a ditch, the others fill the ditch with branches, earth, and stones, so as to raise the bottom in such a way that it can climb out. They fear the cry of the pig <note>This was a common belief in antiquity and the Middle Ages; Pliny the Elder recorded that the squeal of a pig could terrify an elephant.</note>; if they flee, they do no less harm to their own kind than to their enemies with their feet; they love rivers and wander along their banks; their great weight prevents them from swimming. They devour stones and tree trunks. They hate rats. Flies love their scent and land on them; they wrinkle their skin and crush them within the folds.

When they cross rivers, they send their young downstream and stay, themselves, upstream; in this way, they break the current <note>By standing upstream, the larger elephants acted as a breakwater to keep the calves from being swept away.</note>.

->21 r.<-

*The Serpent.* — A very large animal. When it sees any bird in the air, it inhales so strongly that the bird falls into its mouth.
Marcus Regulus <note>Marcus Atilius Regulus (d. c. 250 BCE), a Roman general and consul. This legend of a giant serpent in Africa was popular in classical accounts.</note>, consul of the Roman army, was attacked, along with his army, by such an animal and thought he would perish. A siege engine <note>Original: "machine murale," a catapult or ballista usually used to attack city walls.</note> killed it; it was 125 feet long, that is to say, 64 and a half fathoms, and its head rose above all the greenery of a forest.
This great serpent coils around the legs of the cow and then remains there, and its head as well, until it drains her dry. Of this species, in the time of the Emperor Claudius, one died on the Vatican Hill. Inside its belly was found an entire child that it had swallowed.

It has legs made of a single piece <note>A common medieval misconception, stemming from Caesar and Pliny, that elks lacked joints in their legs and had to sleep leaning against trees.</note> and to sleep it leans against a tree; hunters saw through the tree, it falls over, and they capture it; there is no other way, for it possesses incredible speed in running.

**Bonase** (Bison). — The bison is born in Paeonia <note>An ancient region in the central Balkans, roughly modern-day North Macedonia.</note>. It has the mane of a horse and for the rest resembles a bull, except that its horns are curved backwards and it cannot charge. While fleeing, it drops its dung every four hundred fathoms <note>Original: "brasses," a measurement of length based on the span of a man's arms.</note> and, if one touches this dung, it burns like fire.

Felines keep their claws in their sheaths and only extend them for hunting or defense.

**Lioness.** — She defends her young. To avoid being frightened by the hunting spears <note>Original: "épieux," large spears used specifically for hunting big game.</note>, she keeps her eyes fixed on the ground, so that in her flight her cubs are not taken prisoner.

**Lion.** — This terrible animal fears nothing as much as the noise of empty carts and the crowing of roosters: their combs also frighten him.

**Panther**, in Africa.

She has the form of a lioness, but is taller on her legs, which are thinner and longer, with a white coat marked by black rosettes <note>This likely describes a leopard or panther, which in medieval lore was said to possess a sweet scent and beautiful coat to attract prey</note>: all animals take pleasure in seeing her, and they would remain gathered around her if her face were not so terrifying.

->23 r.<-

Knowing this, she hides her face, and the surrounding animals are reassured and approach to better enjoy such great beauty; then she seizes the nearest one and devours it.

*Camels.* — The Bactrians have two humps, the Arabs one. They are swift in battle and resistant to heavy burdens. This animal, an observer of rule and measure <note>Leonardo highlights the camel's legendary sense of moderation and self-regulation</note>, will not stand up if it is over-loaded, and if the journey is too long, it stops, and the merchants must camp there.

*The Tiger* — is born in Hyrcania <note>An ancient region located southeast of the Caspian Sea, famous in antiquity for its tigers</note>, resembling the panther by the various spots on its skin. It is of an astonishing velocity. The hunter, when he finds the tiger's cubs, takes them, placing mirrors in the spot from which he departs, and flees on a swift horse. The tiger, upon returning, looks at itself in the mirrors placed on the ground, and seeing its own reflection, it believes it is seeing its cubs.

<note>original vocabulary: lioness, rosettes, Camels, Bactrians, burdens, The Tiger, Hyrcania, panther, mirrors</note>

Scratching with its paw, it discovers the deception, and, by scent, it follows the hunter. The hunter releases one of the cubs and the tiger carries it off and returns toward the hunter until the latter boards a boat.

*Catoblepas*. — Native to Ethiopia, near the source of the Niger <note>original: "Negrepont"; Leonardo likely confuses the Negroponte (Euboea) with the Nigris or Niger river mentioned in classical sources regarding this beast</note>, the catoblepas is a medium-sized and sluggish serpent; it has a head so large that it carries it with difficulty and lets it hang down. Without this sluggishness it would be a plague to man, for whoever meets its eyes falls dead.

From the province of Cyrenaica <note>original: "Arénaïque"; referring to the region in modern-day Libya</note>, no longer than twelve fingers, it bears on its head a white spot similar to a diadem. As its whistling drives away every other serpent, by its resemblance of form <note>likely referring to its royal status among snakes</note> it does not move with sinuosity, but remains upright.

->24 v.<-

It is said that a <term>basilisk</term> <note>A legendary reptile reputed to be a king of serpents and said to have the power to cause death with a single glance</note> having died from the spear-thrust of a rider, the venom traveling up the spear killed not the rider but the horse. It spoils the grain and not only that which it touches, but wherever it breathes, it withers the grass and shatters stones.

<summary>Leonardo describes the legendary catoblepas, whose gaze is lethal, and the basilisk, a creature of such potent venom that it can kill through a spear and destroy the very environment around it.</summary>

# THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

*Weasel* <note>original: "Bellette"</note>. — This animal, finding the lair of the basilisk, kills it with the odor of its urine—a scent that many times kills the weasel itself.

*Cerastes*. — They have four small, movable horns. To feed, they hide themselves under leaves; they then move their little horns, which appear to birds like small, wriggling worms. The birds descend to peck at them, and the cerastes wrap around and devour them.

*Amphisbaena*. — It has two heads, one of which is at the tail, as if it were not enough to release venom from just one side.

*Jaculus* <note>original: "Saculum"; referring to the mythical "javelin snake" known for leaping from trees</note>. — It perches in trees, launches itself like a dart, and kills wild animals.

*Asp*. — There is no remedy for its bite except to cut off the bitten part. This pestilential animal loves its mate and never leaves its side. If one dies, the other pursues the killer and is so attentive to its revenge that it overcomes every obstacle.

Passing through an army, it seeks to wound only its enemy; it can only be avoided by fleeing quickly across water. Its eyes are deep-set, it has large ears, and its hearing guides it more than its sight.

### 25 v.

*Ichneumon.* — The mortal enemy of the asp <note>The "asp" likely refers to the Egyptian cobra</note>: it lives in Egypt. When it sees an asp, it runs to roll itself in the mud of the Nile and dries itself in the sun; it thus coats itself with three or four layers of silt as if with a breastplate. Then, it attacks the asp, leaps at its throat, and strangles it.

*Crocodile.* — It has four feet; alone among animals, it has no tongue and bites with the upper jaw. It reaches forty feet in length, has claws, and is clothed in scales that are proof against any blow. By day, it remains on land; it swims at night. Having fed on fish, it falls asleep on the riverbank with its mouth open.

### 26 r.

Then, the wren <note>original: "troglodyte"; Leonardo refers to the Egyptian plover, traditionally called the "trochilus" or "troglodyte" in ancient texts, which was believed to clean the crocodile's teeth</note>, a small bird, leaps between its teeth and pecks at the remains of food there; it picks at him to his great pleasure. He falls asleep; the ichneumon <note>The ichneumon is a species of mongoose</note> then darts into his mouth and pierces his stomach and intestines.

*Of the Dolphin.* — Nature has given animals as well the instinct for what is [beneficial to them] <note>The sentence breaks off here at the end of the page</note>

The dolphin knows the value of the sharp edge of the fins on its spine and that the belly of the crocodile is tender. When they fight, it glides beneath him and slits his belly.

->26 v.<-

*Hippopotamus*. — When it feels sick, it seeks out a thorn or, failing that, a sharp fragment of a reed, and rubs a vein until it opens.

Once the excess blood has flowed out, it closes the wound with mud. It resembles the horse; with a cloven hoof, a twisted tail, the teeth of a wild boar, and a neck with a mane. Its hide can be tanned by wetting it. In the fields, it enters walking backward in order to prepare its escape; it has deep-set eyes, large ears, and guides itself primarily by hearing.

*Ibis*. — It resembles the stork; when sick, it fills its crop with water and gives itself a clyster <note>An enema; Leonardo repeats a common medieval myth that the ibis invented this medical procedure</note> with its beak.

*Stag*. — When it feels itself bitten by the spider called the phallenge <note>original: "phallenge"; likely referring to the phalangium, a type of venomous spider or harvestman mentioned in classical texts</note>, it eats crayfish and is cured.

### 27 r.

*Lizard.* — It eats sow thistle <note>original: "laiteron"; a common weed with milky sap</note> to protect itself against the venom of snakes.

*Swallow.* — Restores sight to its young with the juice of the celandine <note>original: "chelidoine"; the plant's name comes from the Greek word for swallow, *chelidōn*, due to the ancient belief that the birds used its yellow sap to heal their chicks' eyes</note>.

*Weasel.* — It eats rue <note>A bitter herb (Ruta graveolens) frequently cited in ancient texts as an antidote to poison</note> before hunting rats.

*Wild Boar.* — This animal heals itself using ivy.

*Snake.* — To renew itself, it sheds its skin starting from the head; this process takes a day and a night.

*Panther.* — It continues to fight even with its entrails falling out, flinging itself still upon the hunters.

### 27 v.

*Chameleon.* — It takes on the color of whatever it perches upon. It is often devoured by elephants along with the leaves on which it sits <note>This reflects a common myth in medieval bestiaries that the chameleon's camouflage was so successful that grazing animals consumed them by mistake</note>.

*Raven.* — When it has killed a chameleon, it purges itself with laurel.

Moderation restrains all vices.
The ermine prefers to die rather than to soil itself. <note>The ermine was a Renaissance symbol of purity and moderation; it was said that the animal would rather be captured by hunters than stain its white fur with mud.</note>
The rooster does not crow if it does not first flap its wings three times.

The parrot, passing from one branch to another, does not place its foot where it has not first placed its beak.

The vow is born from the death of hope. <note>A characteristically cynical or stoic observation by Leonardo on the nature of human promises.</note>
Movement follows the center of weight.

->60 recto<-

Nature has granted suffering to animal souls endowed with movement for the preservation of those organs which might otherwise be diminished or damaged. Vegetative souls, <note>In the Aristotelian tradition Leonardo followed, the "vegetative soul" governs growth and nutrition but lacks the "sensitive soul's" ability to feel pain or move.</note> being without movement, do not have to struggle against opposing objects; therefore, suffering is not necessary in plants. If they are broken, they do not feel pain in the same manner as animals.

->60 verso<-

Envy wounds with an infamous feint—that is to say, by slandering—an act which strikes terror into virtue.

->61 recto<-

A good reputation flies and rises to heaven,

## 77 r.

The water that springs from the mountain is the blood
that gives it life. If one of its veins opens upon
its flanks, Nature, who aids her living beings,
eager to repair the lack of the moisture
that has flowed out, abounds there with a curious haste,
just as it does at the place where a man has been struck.
One sees then, by this aid, the blood
multiply under the skin in the manner of a swelling—

## 77 v. <note>Folio 77 verso</note>

The water that flows near the bottom, between the rocks, will be slower than the rest, because of the percussions <note>impacts or turbulence</note> caused by the currents.

The air is azure because of the darkness that it has above it, because black and white make azure <note>Leonardo's theory of color suggested that the blue of the sky resulted from the white light of the sun illuminating the dark void of space through the atmosphere</note>.

Violent movements weaken in proportion to their distance from their causes.

You who buy water by the ounce <term>once: a historical unit of measurement for water flow</term>, know that you can be greatly mistaken. Indeed, if you take an ounce in still water and an ounce in running water, against the three of your ounce, one ounce near the surface, one near the bottom, one across the thread of the water <note>Leonardo warns that the volume of water delivered depends on pressure and velocity, not just the size of the opening</note>.

As much as a natural movement <note>such as a falling object</note> separates from its starting point, so much faster it becomes.

### 81 r. <note>Folio 81 recto</note>

No surface will appear perfect if the eye is not equally distant from its extremities. <note>Leonardo is describing the optical distortion that occurs when a surface is viewed from an angle rather than from a central, perpendicular vantage point.</note>

### 81 to 85 and 86 r. <note>Folios 81 to 85 and 86 recto</note>

Everything appears larger at midnight than at noon, and in the morning than at noon. <note>This relates to Leonardo's studies on irradiation, where a light source appears larger against a dark background.</note>

The pupil of the owl is larger than that of a man and it sees more than a man at night; at noon, it sees nothing, and at night it sees things larger than by day.

Estimate for the decoration of a hall:
The <term>vaulting</term> <note>original: "voussure"; the curved structure of the ceiling</note> 30.
For the <term>molding</term> <note>original: "gorge"; a concave architectural molding</note> below, I estimate each panel at 7 <term>livres</term> <note>a French currency unit used by the translator to approximate Leonardo's original Italian lire</note> in expenses; and for the <term>azure</term> <note>a bright blue pigment, often made from lapis lazuli</note> as well as stones, gold, <term>white lead</term> <note>original: "blanc de céruse"; a common but toxic white pigment used in Renaissance painting</note>, plaster, indigo, and glue, 3 livres; in time, 3 days' work.
The <term>histories</term> <note>narrative scenes or figurative paintings</note> under these walls with their <term>pilasters</term> <note>rectangular columns attached to a wall</note>, 12 livres.
I estimate the expense for enamel, azure, gold, and other colors at 1 1/2 livres.
