# MANUSCRIPT I

*The tenth volume of the Arconati collection, consisting of notebooks of 16 leaves numbered from 1 to 48, then from 2 to 91, totaling 141 leaves.* <note>Galeazzo Arconati was a 17th-century collector who owned many of Leonardo's manuscripts before donating them to the Biblioteca Ambrosiana.</note>

*The cover bears no markings in Leonardo's hand. In 1497, Leonardo completed the decoration of the "Sala delle Asse" <note>The "Room of the Wooden Boards," a room in the Sforza Castle in Milan decorated with a magnificent trompe-l'œil of interlocking trees.</note> at the Castle of Milan.*

---

->1 to 12 recto<-

*Geometry and mathematics, of no interest.* <note>This likely reflects the assessment of a previous cataloger or editor regarding the technical diagrams on these pages.</note>

->12 verso<-

All the branches of trees, at every stage of their height, are equal <note>in total cross-sectional area</note> to the thickness of their trunk.

All the ramifications of waters, at every stage of their length, provided they have an equal movement <term>equal movement: constant velocity</term>, are equal to the thickness of their source.

<summary>This page introduces Manuscript I of Leonardo da Vinci's notebooks, transitioning from geometric exercises to his famous "Rule of Trees" regarding the proportional thickness of branches and water systems.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Arconati Collection, Sala delle Asse, Milan, botany, hydraulics, tree branching, fluid dynamics</keywords>

->13 recto and verso<-

# Mathematics.

->14 recto<-

# Water Conduits.

->14 verso<-

If someone descends, step by step, making a jump from one to the next, and you add up all the forces of percussion <term>percussion: the impact or force of a collision</term> and the weight of such jumps, you will find that they are equal to the total percussion and weight that this man would produce if he fell perpendicularly, headfirst, from the height of the said staircase.

->15 recto<-

He who does not value life does not deserve it.

->15 verso to 17 recto<-

# Mathematics.

->18 recto<-

Nature is full of infinite reasons <note>original: "ragioni" — here meaning logical principles or laws of cause and effect</note> which were never in experience. <note>Leonardo suggests that nature operates on laws that exist independently of whether humans have yet observed or tested them through experiment.</note>

<vocab>Mathematics, Water Conduits, percussion, Leonardo da Vinci, experience</vocab>

### 48 r.

> The dark colors of mountains, at a great distance, take on an azure <note>original: "azur." This refers to "atmospheric perspective," a technique Leonardo pioneered to show depth by making distant objects appear bluer and less distinct.</note> more beautiful and more pure than their illuminated parts: when the rock of the mountain turns red, its lit parts are unified, and the more it is illuminated, the more it takes on its own natural color.

### 48 v.

> The gestures for paintings are best observed among players of ball, tennis <note>original: "paume." Specifically "jeu de paume," an early form of tennis played with the hand or a racket.</note>, or pall-mall <note>original: "mail." A precursor to croquet and golf where a ball was struck with a mallet through a hoop.</note>, when they struggle together, better than in any other place or action. <note>Leonardo frequently hung around public spaces and sports to sketch the tension and mechanics of the human body in motion.</note>

### 49 v.

> The more an object approaches the eye, the larger its visual angle appears; reflection does the opposite; the more one measures it close to the eye, the more it decreases.

> The bee can be compared to fraud; it has honey in its mouth and venom in its sting <note>original: "cul." Literally "backside" or "tail," used here to describe the stinger.</note>.

*Turks and Germans.* — The generation of men will come to the point of not understanding one another's speech.
<note>A riddle referring to the diversity of languages and the lack of a universal tongue.</note>

*Flies.* — Men shall emerge from the tombs transformed into insects and shall assault men, taking food from their very hands and tables.
<note>Leonardo describes the life cycle of flies, which develop as larvae in organic matter (the "tombs") and then fly out to pester humans at mealtime.</note>

*Laborers.* — There are many who will skin their mother and turn back her hide.
<note>A riddle for plowing the earth; "Mother Earth" is being turned over by the plowshare.</note>

*Readings.* — Happy are those who lend an ear to the words of the dead.
<note>This refers to the act of reading books written by authors who have long since passed away.</note>

->64 r.<-

*Mattresses.* — Feathers will lift men, like birds, toward the sky.
<note>A reference to sleeping on feather beds, which elevate the sleeper off the hard ground.</note>

*Swords.* — The work of men's hands becomes the cause of their death.

*Misery.* — Men will pursue the very thing they dread the most.
<note>This likely refers to the pursuit of wealth or money, which brings the misery and greed that people otherwise fear.</note>

*Papyrus.* — Things once separated will be joined together and receive such power that they shall restore lost memory to men.
<note>original: "papyrus"</note>
<note>This refers to the manufacturing of paper or papyrus from individual plant fibers; once joined into a sheet, they can be used to record history.</note>

(Paper made from separated weight serves to write history).

*Dice.* — One will see the bones of the dead deciding...
<note>A reference to gambling with dice, which were historically carved from animal bones.</note>

<page-num>215</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

rapidly from the fortune of the one who moves them.
<note>This completes the riddle for "Dice" from the previous page; the dice determine the gambler's wealth as they are thrown.</note>

*Lantern.* — The oxen will defend with their horns the fire of its death.
<note>In Leonardo’s time, lantern panes were often made of thin, translucent slices of ox horn to protect the candle flame (the "fire of its death") from the wind.</note>

*Axe handle.* — The forest will bring to light young ones who will cause its death.
<note>The wooden handle of an axe is made from a young tree or branch taken from the forest, which is then used to chop down the rest of the trees.</note>

*Grain.* — Men will harshly beat the support of their life.
<note>This refers to the threshing of wheat or grain, where the stalks—the source of bread and thus life—are beaten to separate the seeds.</note>

*Balls.* — Animal skins will make men shout, run, and swear.
<note>A reference to ball games played with inflated leather bladders or stuffed skins; the competitive nature of the game provokes strong emotions.</note>

*Comb.* — The disunited thing will be the cause of union.
<note>The teeth of a comb are separate ("disunited"), but they work together to gather and untangle hair into an orderly "union."</note>

*Bagpipes.* — The wind, passing through the skin of animals, will make men dance.
<note>Bagpipes are constructed from an animal skin bag that holds air (wind) to produce music.</note>

*Walnut trees.* — Those who have done the best will be struck down and their children taken away, flayed and stripped and their bones broken and smashed.
<note>Leonardo describes the harvest of walnuts: the trees are beaten with poles to drop the nuts (the "children"), which are then husked (flayed) and their shells (bones) cracked open to reach the meat.</note>

*Crucifix.* — O me! What do I see! The Savior crucified again.
<note>This likely refers to the manufacture or the commercial sale of many crucifixes, or perhaps the way religious symbols are treated as mere objects.</note>

*Mouth.* — Great noise will come out of the tombs of those who died a bad and violent death.
<note>A dark riddle about eating meat. The "tombs" are the stomachs of humans who have eaten animals that were slaughtered ("violent death"); the "noise" is the human voice speaking or shouting from that body.</note>

*Manuscript.* — The more one speaks with skins clothed in feeling, the more knowledge one will acquire.
<note>This refers to reading books made of parchment (animal skin) which contain the thoughts and sentiments of authors.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a series of Leonardo da Vinci’s "prophecies," which are clever riddles that describe everyday objects—like lanterns, bagpipes, and books—using apocalyptic or violent imagery. These riddles showcase Leonardo's habit of looking at common items from a strange, detached, and highly metaphorical perspective.</summary>
<keywords>riddles, prophecies, daily life, tools, harvesting, parchment, musical instruments, metaphors</keywords>

*Priests.* — Almost all the tabernacles where the *Body of the Lord* <note>original: "Corpus Domini"; the consecrated bread or Host in the Catholic Eucharist</note> is kept will be seen to move clearly of their own accord along the various roads of the world. <note>Leonardo refers to the practice of carrying the Host in processions.</note>

->66 r.<-

*Tallow.* — And those who graze upon the grass shall turn the night into day. <note>A riddle referring to candles made from the fat (tallow) of grazing animals like cows or sheep.</note>

*Representations of Planets.* — Many terrestrial and aquatic animals will ascend among the stars. <note>Refers to the constellations of the zodiac named after animals, such as Taurus the bull or Pisces the fish.</note>

*The Oven.* — From many, food shall be snatched from their very mouths.

*Furnaces.* — From those who have their mouths filled by the hands of others, their food shall be taken away. <note>Likely referring to the use of a baker's shovel to retrieve bread from a hot oven.</note>

->66 v.<-

*Crucifixes.* — I see Christ being sold and crucified once again, and his saints being martyred. <note>A critique of the sale of religious art, crucifixes, and perhaps the corruption of the Church (simony).</note>

*Medicine.* — Men will reach such a state of wretchedness that they will allow others to profit from their sufferings; from this comes the loss of their true wealth: their health. <note>A cynical observation on the medical profession of the Renaissance, where doctors earned a living from the sick.</note>

*Saints.* — Those who have been dead for a thousand years will pay the expenses of many of the living. <note>Leonardo refers to the cult of relics and the income generated by shrines dedicated to ancient saints.</note>

# THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

# DRAWINGS

## 24 recto and verso, 25 recto

*Research into heraldic forms based on plant life (24 r.), shield shapes (24 r.), and shell forms (25 r.).* <note>Recto (r.) and verso (v.) refer to the front and back of a manuscript leaf.</note>

## 96 recto

*Head of a dog for the study of proportion.* <note>Leonardo frequently applied geometric principles to the anatomy of animals to find universal rules of beauty and scale.</note>

## 136 verso

*Drawn obliquely to the text regarding the proportions of movement: a noble and weary figure, which some have suggested represents the master himself.* <note>original: "celle du maître." This refers to the long-standing debate among historians as to whether certain sketches in the margins of Leonardo's scientific notes are self-portraits.</note>

# MANUSCRIPT L

## WRITTEN IN 1502

The page numbering is not in the author's hand <note>original: "autographe"</note>. It is designated as the 2nd by Oltrocchi and "L" by Venturi <note>Baldassarre Oltrocchi and Jean-Baptiste Venturi were pioneering scholars who organized and cataloged Leonardo’s scattered notebooks</note>.

In 1502, Leonardo was serving as the military engineer for Cesare Borgia <note>the ambitious Duke of Valentinois who was attempting to conquer the Romagna region of Italy</note>.

Ludovico the Moor <note>Ludovico Sforza, the Duke of Milan and Leonardo’s former patron</note> has been a prisoner for life in the castle of Loches since 1500. Leonardo left Milan on December 15, 1499, and began his wandering life, passing through Mantua, Venice, Florence, Umbria, and the Marches.

---

Inside front cover:
Paul of Vanocco in Siena.
The small room, upstairs, for the apostles.

The quill must necessarily be accompanied by the penknife, because one without the other is worth very little <note>original: "La plume a nécessaire compagnie du canif parce que l'un sans l'autre ne vaut guère"</note> (1).

Buildings by Bramante <note>Donato Bramante, the renowned Renaissance architect and a close associate of Leonardo</note>.
The castellan <note>the governor or caretaker of a castle</note> taken prisoner.

->72 r.<-

Flax is dedicated to death and to the corruption of mortals; to death by the snares and nets for birds and fish; to corruption by the linen cloths in which the dead are wrapped when they are buried, and who decay within the said cloths. <note>Leonardo often sought symbolic meanings in natural processes; here he links the utility of the flax plant to the end of life.</note>

Furthermore, flax only detaches from its stalks <note>original: "fétus," the woody core of the flax plant</note> when it begins to soak and decay. <note>This refers to "retting," a process where flax is submerged in water to rot the stalks so the fibers can be extracted.</note> It is therefore fitting for crowning and adorning funeral rites.

### 76 v. 77

#### Works at Cesena. <note>Cesena was the capital of Cesare Borgia's short-lived Duchy of Romagna. Leonardo arrived here in the summer of 1502 to inspect and improve the city’s fortifications and canal systems.</note>

### 77 v.

If I find myself in a position at sea equally distant between the shore and the mountain, the shore appears to be much larger than the mountain. <note>Leonardo is noting an optical illusion or a principle of perspective where the horizontal expanse of a nearby coastline dominates the visual field more than a distant vertical mountain height.</note>

### 78 r.

One should create a harmony with various falling waters, just as you saw at the fountain of Rimini, on the 8th day of August 1502. <note>Leonardo refers here to the *Fontana della Pigna* in Rimini. He was captivated by the musical quality of water striking the basin, suggesting that the different heights and volumes of falling water could be "tuned" like a musical instrument.</note>

### 78 v.

...and Marliano. *On Calculation*, by Albertus Magnus.
<note>original: "De calculatione." Giovanni Marliani was a famous physician and mathematician. Albertus Magnus was a medieval scholar whose works on natural science Leonardo studied deeply.</note>

Someone, seeing a woman ready to offer a shield in a joust, looked at the buckler and cried out at the sight of her lance: "Alas, there is too small a worker for so large a shop!"
<note>This is a bawdy double entendre typical of Leonardo’s occasional ribald humor; the "worker" and "shop" are sexual metaphors used in the context of a tournament.</note>

->Folio 1 recto<-

Begun in Milan, on the 12th day of September 1508.

Death in the elderly, occurring without fever, is caused by the veins which, running from the spleen to the liver, thicken so much that they eventually close and no longer allow passage for the blood.
<note>Leonardo was a pioneer in the study of anatomy through dissection; here he describes what we now understand as the hardening of the arteries or vascular decay due to aging.</note>

The hollows of the veins of the earth, through the continuous flow of water, are constantly widening.
<note>Leonardo frequently compared the "body" of the Earth to the human body, seeing water as the "blood" of the planet flowing through subterranean "veins."</note>

The roots of willows do not allow the banks of canals to be destroyed; the branches, nourished through the thickness of the dikes and then trimmed low, thicken every year and produce continuous shoots: in this way, you have a living dike made of a single piece.
<note>A practical observation on hydraulic engineering, suggesting a method of "bio-engineering" to stabilize man-made waterways using the natural growth of trees.</note>

->Folio 1 verso<-

The surface of every opaque body participates in the color of its object.
<note>A fundamental rule of Leonardo’s optical theory: he observed that the color we see on an object is never "pure" but is always influenced by the reflected light and color of the objects surrounding it.</note>

gives them this appearance, for many are a great many times larger than the earth. Think of what our earth would look like, seen from such a great distance, and consider how many stars one could place in width and in length between those that shine in the dark space.

I cannot resist blaming those ancients who said that the sun is no larger than it appears: among them was Epicurus. <note>Epicurus (c. 341–270 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who famously argued that celestial bodies were roughly the size they appeared to the human eye.</note> Such reasoning comes from a light placed in the air equidistant from the center; it does not appear to diminish in size, at any distance whatsoever.

Regarding the reasons for the size and power <note>original: "vertu," referring here to the sun's physical and generative influence.</note> of the sun, I reserve them for the fourth book; but I am quite amazed that Socrates described this celestial body by comparing it to a burning stone, and he who drew him out of this error was not wrong. <note>Leonardo likely refers to the trial of Anaxagoras, who was accused of impiety for claiming the sun was a red-hot mass of metal larger than the Peloponnese; Socrates was often associated with these views in popular accounts.</note> But I would like to find words to blame those who wish to worship men instead of the sun. For I do not see in the universe a body of greater dignity and excellence than that one, which illuminates all the others.

...celestial bodies. All souls descend from it <note>Leonardo is referring to the sun, which he views as the spiritual and physical engine of the world.</note>, for the heat found in living animals comes from their souls. There is, in the whole universe, no other heat or light than its own, as I shall demonstrate in the fourth book. Those who have wished to worship men as gods—Jupiter, Saturn, Mars, and the like—have committed a great error, since we see that a man, even if he were as large as the earth, would appear like a tiny star that looks like a mere point in the universe; and we see these same men, prone to rot and decay, within their graves.

->5 v.<- <note>Folio 5 verso.</note>

The stars are visible at night because we are beneath the thickness of the air, which is full of infinite particles of moisture. Each of these particles, when struck by a solar ray, shines brightly; without this humid air, we would see the stars even at high noon. <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that the atmosphere scatters sunlight (Rayleigh scattering), creating a "curtain" of light that hides the stars during the day.</note>

Many make a business of impostures and false miracles, deceiving the multitude; and if someone were to denounce their deceits, they would "sting" <note>original: "poncano," an old Italianism for "pungere" (to prick/sting), followed by "poindraient." Leonardo suggests that those who expose fraud are often met with physical or social retaliation.</note> him.

### 6 r.

Perhaps <term>Epicurus</term><note>An ancient Greek philosopher (c. 341–270 BC) who argued that the sun was exactly the size it appeared to be to the human eye.</note> saw the shadows of columns on opposite walls that were equal to the diameter of the actual column. Based on the convergence of the parallel shadow, from its beginning to its end, he judged that the sun—also offering such parallelism—was consequently no wider than that column. He did not realize that such a lack of shadow-thinning was constant relative to the extreme distance of the sun.

If the sun were smaller than the earth, the stars and a portion of our hemisphere would be without light.

### 6 v. 7 r. and v. 8 r.

### Hydraulics.

### 8 v.

Epicurus says that the sun has the size that it appears to have: now, it appears to be one foot <note>original: "d’un pied"</note>; we must therefore hold it to be so. In this case, the moon would obscure the sun; however, the moon being smaller, it would be less than a foot, and when our world obscures the moon, it would be

# THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI
<page-num>256</page-num>

The water that would flow from this land revealed by the receding sea would begin to form streams in the lower sections; these, by carving into the ground, would receive the surrounding waters. In this way, their beds would widen, and after the waters had drained away, the hollows would become torrents during times of rain. These waters would continue to flow until the spaces between them became mounds; then, as the water drained away, these mounds would begin to dry and to form strata <note>Layers of sedimentary rock.</note> in layers according to the thickness of the silt <note>original: "fanges" — mud or mire</note> that the rivers had carried into the sea during their floods.

### Sphericity of the elements.

->27 r.<-

### Shape of the elements.

Against those who deny the opinion of Plato, <note>Plato argued in his dialogue "Timaeus" that the four elements were composed of specific geometric solids (e.g., Earth as a cube).</note> saying that if one or the other took on this shape, a vacuum would be produced, which is not true.

It is not necessary for the elements that clothe <note>original: "revêtent"; meaning to envelop or surround, as water surrounds the earth.</note> one another to be of equal thickness between the part that clothes and the part that is clothed. We see the sphere of water presenting different thicknesses from its surface to the bottom; and yet, it would cover the cubic form of the earth if it had the shape of a cube—that is, eight corners—but it covers the

<term>Plato</term> <term>elements</term> <term>sphericity</term> <term>cubic</term> <term>earth</term> <term>water</term> <term>motion</term> <term>solar rays</term> <term>celestial bodies</term>

<summary>Leonardo concludes his optical observations on celestial bodies and begins a geometric defense of the elements, arguing that fluid elements like water can perfectly envelop solid elements like a cubic Earth without leaving a vacuum.</summary>

<sig>27 v.</sig>

earth, having countless angles of reefs covered with water. 

Regarding the air that clothes <note>original: "revêt"; Leonardo often uses the metaphor of "clothing" to describe how one element surrounds or envelops another.</note> the sphere of water, the mountains, and the valleys: no vacuum remains between the earth and the air. 

And Plato answers me that the surface of the figures <note>Referring to the geometric shapes Plato assigned to the elements in his dialogue *Timaeus* (e.g., the cube for earth).</note> that the elements would have would not be durable. 

Every flexible and liquid element has, of necessity, a spherical surface. 

A thing is "high" which moves away from the center, and "low" which approaches the center. <term>center</term> <note>In the Aristotelian and Leonardo's worldview, the "center of the world" refers to the center of the Earth, which acts as the center of gravity.</note> Water does not move unless it descends, and in moving, it descends. These concepts serve me to prove that water—which does not move by its own power—has its surface equidistant to the center of the world (not speaking of drops nor small quantities that attract one another, like steel to its <term>filings</term> <note>original: "limaille"; Leonardo here recognizes surface tension and magnetism as exceptions to his general rule of gravity.</note>) but of large quantities.

### Of the 5 regular bodies.

Against those commentators who vituperate <note>To vituperate means to blame or find fault with. Leonardo is defending the foundational status of ancient Greek and Roman science against his contemporaries who dismissed them.</note> the ancient inventors, who created grammars and sciences, and who attack the inven- <note>The word "inventions" or "inventors" is cut off at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>Leonardo argues that liquid elements naturally form spherical surfaces relative to the Earth's center of gravity to prevent a vacuum. He also begins a defense of ancient scientists against contemporary critics regarding the geometry of the five regular Platonic solids.</summary>

deceased commentators. Why have they not known how to invent in their turn? Because of their laziness; they produce so many books only to continually criticize their masters with false arguments.

They call the earth tetrahedral <note>original: "tétracedronique"; likely a transcription error for "tétraédrique" or a variation on the term for a four-sided solid.</note>, hexahedral, or cubic—a body with six bases—and they prove it by saying that among the regular bodies, none is more stable than the cube. They attributed to fire the form of a tetrahedron or pyramid, the pyramid being more mobile: yet the cube is more mobile, beyond comparison, than the pyramid.

The more sides a body has, the easier its movement is: the triangle <note>Here Leonardo refers to the triangular-faced pyramid.</note> is of slower movement than the cube, and consequently, it was necessary to attribute the pyramid to the earth, and not the cube.

# THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

If the lines of sight converged at an angle toward a mathematical point—which is invisible—then all objects would appear as one single thing, and this thing would be indivisible; there would no longer be any interval between one star and another. However, experience shows us all things separated by proportionate and intelligible spaces. Furthermore, this faculty where the <term>species</term> <note>In Renaissance optics, "species" refers to the formal images or "likenesses" emitted by objects that travel through the air to the eye.</note> of things are imprinted is itself divisible into as many parts, large or small, as there are <term>simulacra</term> <note>Simulacra are the visual representations or "idols" of objects perceived by the senses.</note> of things seen. We shall conclude, therefore, that the sense receives the images that are reflected on the surface of the eye and then judges them within; therefore, they do not converge at a single point, nor consequently at a single angle.

->34 v.<-

They say that the sun is not hot because it is not the color of fire, but whiter and clearer. To those people, one can respond that when liquefied bronze is at its hottest, it most resembles the color of the sun, and as it cools, it takes on the color of fire.

<summary>Leonardo argues against the idea that vision converges into a single mathematical point, asserting instead that the eye must have a divisible surface to perceive space, and compares the sun's heat to molten metal.</summary>
<keywords>optics, vision, geometry of the eye, solar heat, molten bronze, perception of stars, species, simulacra</keywords>

### 56 v.

### On the friction of the heavens, whether it produces sound or not.

Sound results from the blow of a hard body striking the air, and if it is made between two bodies, it is by means of the air. It would follow that the heavens do not produce sound in rubbing against one another, for lack of air between them: if this friction had taken place, throughout so many centuries during which they have turned, they would have been worn away by their immense daily speed. If they did produce a sound, this sound could not spread, since sound produced under water is little heard and would be heard less or not at all in dense bodies; the friction of polished bodies makes no noise: thus there would be none from the friction of the heavens. If the heavens have not been polished by friction, they are globular and rough, and therefore their contact is not continuous, and a vacuum is produced—the vacuum which does not exist in nature. Therefore, friction would have consumed the boundaries of each heaven, and especially toward the middle rather than toward the poles, since at the middle the motion is faster: and then the friction and the sound would cease— <note>Leonardo is refuting the ancient Pythagorean concept of the "Music of the Spheres," arguing through physics and mechanics that the celestial bodies cannot produce audible sound.</note>

<sig>25</sig>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, friction, heavens, sound, vacuum, daily speed</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo argues against the existence of the "Music of the Spheres," using mechanical principles to prove that the friction of celestial bodies in a vacuum cannot produce sound and would otherwise destroy them.</summary>
<keywords>celestial mechanics, acoustics, vacuum, Music of the Spheres, friction, astronomy</keywords>

### 57 r.

They say that the stars have their own light <note>Intrinsic or self-generated light.</note>, alleging that if this were not so for Venus and Mercury, when they pass between our eye and the sun, they would obscure as much of the sun as they cover from our sight.

This is false; a dark object <note>original: "l'ombreux"</note> placed within a luminous field is surrounded by external rays and remains invisible. When one views the sun through branches from a great distance, the branches do not appear to block any part of the sun. The same is seen with the planets which, although they have no brilliance of their own, do not mask any part of the sun from our view.

They say that the stars shine all the more when they are high above us, and that if they did not have their own light, the shadow cast by the earth between them and the sun would obscure them, as these stars would not see the solar body and would not be seen by it. They have not considered that the pyramidal shadow <term>pyramidal shadow: the cone-shaped shadow cast by a spherical body like the Earth into space</term> of the earth

<header>THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### The Formation of Fossils.

The floods of silt-laden rivers discharged the animals living beneath the waters near the sea shores; and they were imprinted in the silt and became mired there, dying under the weight and through the deprivation of their ordinary food.

When the sea receded and the waters flowed away, the silt hardened and became stone, and the shells were found encrusted; and through the opening of the shells the silt had penetrated, and these shells remained between the petrification <note>The process of turning into stone</note> that enclosed them and that which they contained.

->79 v.<-

As the riverbeds lowered, these animals—covered by those muds which had consumed their flesh—no longer kept their bones; their decomposed organism had fallen to the bottom of the cavity of their imprint, and the viscous mud had petrified: thus, even animals that have left their imprint and their bones there are preserved.

->80 r.<-

The animal that lives in a shell increases <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing to describe the growth of mollusks or the layering of shells.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the scientific process of fossilization, attributing it to river silt burying marine life which then hardens into stone as sea levels change. He correctly identifies that fossils are the remains of once-living organisms rather than inorganic accidents.</summary>
<keywords>paleontology, fossil formation, sedimentation, petrification, marine biology, geology, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s argument against the astrological or "miraculous" origins of fossils, focusing on the physical evidence of shell growth, river transport, and the specific characteristics of marine debris found on mountaintops.</meta>

<page-num>277</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

its house. The interior, clean and delicate, shows an attachment (an umbilication) <note>A navel-like connection point</note> with the animal, which is marked and suited to receive the junction of the muscles with which it can shut itself inside its house.

->80 v.<-

If you attribute the shells found in the mountains to the constellations <note>A reference to the contemporary belief that fossils were formed by the influence of stars rather than biological processes</note>, how will you explain their differences in size, age, and species in the same location?

How will you explain the gravel brought from various lands by the rivers and mixed together?

Gravel is nothing but pieces of stone that have lost their sharp angles through long tumbling <note>original: "évolution"</note>.

You will see on the mountains seaweed, sea grass, and the remains of sea crayfish.

->82 v.<-

Proof that the sphere of water is round. <note>Leonardo was fascinated by the geometry of the Earth and the way water naturally seeks a spherical form due to gravity.</note>

->83 r.<-

A man who walks travels faster with his head than with his feet; if he walks on level ground, he first leans forward, then just as much backward. <note>Leonardo is observing the shifting center of gravity during the human gait; to move forward, one must first tip the upper body out of balance.</note>

->83 v.<-

**Gravity.**

->84 r.<-

Some say that vapors similar to clouds rise from the moon. If this were so, the spots would be stable neither in position nor in shape. <note>Leonardo argues that the "Man in the Moon" (the lunar maria) cannot be atmospheric clouds because they never change their shape or location.</note>

->84 v.<-

Others say that the moon is composed of transparent matter like **alabaster**, **crystal**, or **glass**. The sun striking the less transparent part would remain on the surface.

This opinion pleased Aristotle <note>The ancient Greek philosopher whose "geocentric" views on the perfection of celestial spheres were still the standard in the Renaissance.</note>, and yet it is false, for we would see its spots...

<summary>Leonardo examines the mechanics of human balance and movement before shifting to astronomical observations, where he refutes Aristotle's theories by arguing that the moon's surface is solid and opaque rather than transparent or atmospheric.</summary>

<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...vary, alternately dark or bright: dark when the sun is in the West and the moon in the middle of the sky, and bright at the full moon, when, being in the East, it faces the sky to the West. At that time, the sun would illuminate the very depths of such transparencies and would abolish every shadow. <note>Leonardo is arguing that if the moon were a transparent sphere, the internal shadows or "spots" we see would change or vanish entirely depending on the angle of the sunlight passing through it.</note>

->85 r.<-

It is also said that the spots come from varying densities, but during eclipses, the solar rays would penetrate these densities. <note>He suggests that if the "Man in the Moon" were simply made of denser material within a translucent whole, the sun's light would still shine through it during an eclipse, which is not what observers see.</note>

Others say that the moon is a mirror that reflects the image of the Earth; a false opinion, for the Earth, where it is not covered by water, has various shapes. When the moon is in the West, it would reflect different spots than when it is in the East; yet the spots of the moon never vary during its movement over our hemisphere. <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that the lunar surface remains constant to the observer. If the moon were a mirror reflecting Earth, the image would shift as the moon traveled over different continents and oceans.</note>

->85 v.<-

A glass sphere filled with cold water reflects rays even hotter than fire. <note>Leonardo is describing the "burning glass" effect. He is intrigued by the fact that a cold medium (water) can concentrate light to such a degree that it generates heat.</note>

->86 r.<-

If you were to say that the cold tempers the rays... <note>Leonardo likely intends to address a potential counter-argument: the idea that the "coldness" of the moon or the water would reduce the heat of the light rays.</note>

<summary>Leonardo continues his scientific inquiry into the moon, using logical deduction to reject the ideas that it is transparent or a mirror reflecting Earth. He also makes notes on the physics of optics, specifically how lenses can concentrate heat.</summary>

## WRITTEN FROM 1513 TO 1514

->RUBRICATED (*Notebook*), ORIGINAL BINDING<-

*In 1513, Leonardo passed through Florence and left Milan on September 24th for Rome, where he sketched the Saint Jerome now held in the Vatican Gallery <note>original: Pinacothèque</note>; he traveled to Parma and was in Florence in December.*

Cover:
The rainbow is neither in the rain, nor in the eye that sees it, although it is born from the rain, the sun, and the eye.
With the sun being in the east and the rain in the west, the bow is generated within the western rain.

Tullius <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and philosopher</note> says that astrology was invented 57,000 years before the Trojan War. <note>original: "Tullius... dicit astrologiam inventam esse annis 57,000 ante bellum Troianum."</note>

The largest cone that can be extracted from a cube is one-third the volume of said cube <note>original: "Triple est le plus grand cône," meaning the cube is triple the cone's volume</note>, and the lateral surface of the cube is double that of the cone.

The circle is equal to a quadrilateral made from half the diameter of that circle multiplied by half of the circumference of the same circle. <note>This is a geometric method for calculating the area of a circle, equivalent to the formula Area = radius × (circumference / 2).</note>

### 3 r.

Note in movements and attitudes how the limbs vary according to the emotions: the shoulder blades <note>original: "omoplates"</note> in the play of the arms and shoulders modify the spine: you will find the causes of this in my book of anatomy.

Observe, you who reproduce the works of nature, the quantity, quality, light, and shadow of every muscle, and note toward which muscle they move, aligned with the center of their axis.

### 3 v.

Three kinds of light illuminate opaque bodies: first, the sun, the window, or fire; the second is universal, as happens in foggy weather; the third is composite, when the sun, in the evening or morning, is entirely below the horizon.

### 4 r.

The background <note>original: "le champ," referring to the field or space surrounding a subject</note> that surrounds a painted figure must be darker than the illuminated part of the figure and lighter than its shaded part.

8 v.

## Theorems.

12 r.

### Order of the first book of water.

Define height and depth <note>original: "bas-fond," referring to the seabed or the shallowest part of a body of water</note>, what dense weight and liquid weight are, and first of all, what constitutes weight and lightness.

Describe why water moves and how it ends its motion, and how it always descends, being bordered by air lower than itself; how the heat of the sun raises it and how it falls back as rain; why water springs from the mountains; and how the water of the equatorial <note>original: "équinoxiales," referring to the regions near the equator</note> seas is higher than the northern water, because the latter is colder. <note>Leonardo correctly intuiting that water expands when heated, meaning the warmer tropical waters would have a higher volume/level than the colder northern waters.</note>

12 v. 13 r. 13 v. 14 r.

### Conduits for water.

--- Page 320 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's observations on mechanics and optics, transitioning from the physical casting of objects to the physics of the human body and the geometry of vision.</meta>

# THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

->14 v.<-

### Mold and casting of an arch. <note>Leonardo frequently used the term "arc" or "arch" to refer to both architectural structures and the curved components of weaponry or machinery.</note>

->15 r.<-

When a man is moving a weight, pay close attention to the lines of his movements from bottom to top. Observe this in one who, stooping down, takes up a weight as he straightens himself; or likewise in one who drags something backward, or pushes it forward, or pulls it downward with a rope.

The weight of the man exerts as much force as the center of his gravity is displaced from the center of his support <note>Leonardo is describing the principle of leverage and balance: the further one leans away from their feet, the more their body weight contributes to the pull or push</note>: to which is added the force of the legs and the bent spine as it straightens.

->15 v.<-

It is possible for the eye not to see distant objects as excessively diminished, as occurs in natural perspective. In that case, the spaces diminish according to the curvature of the eye, which intersects the pyramids <note>Leonardo uses "pyramids" to describe the cone of light or lines of sight traveling from an object to the eye</note> between spherical right angles.

The art that I teach here in the margin intersects these pyramids at right angles upon the surface of the pupil. <note>Leonardo is distinguishing between "natural perspective" (how the curved eye actually perceives) and "artificial perspective" (how a painter projects a 3D scene onto a flat surface).</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanics of the human body when lifting or pulling weights and proposes a geometric theory of perspective that accounts for the curvature of the eye.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, center of gravity, human motion, optics, natural perspective, visual pyramids, geometry of vision</keywords>

--- Page 321 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of optics and perspective, specifically contrasting natural vision with artistic representation, and transitions into his anatomical studies of movement.</meta>

# THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

The pupil, being convex, reflects our entire hemisphere, and yet this will show only a single star; where many small stars are reflected on the surface of the pupil, only one will appear, but larger: thus the moon appears larger and its spots more distinct.

->16 r.<-

Nature shows us an object at a size relative to its distance. My invention forces the spectator to place their eye at a <term>soupirail</term> <note>original: "soupirail"; literally a "breathing hole" or vent, here referring to a small aperture or peephole used to fix the viewer's vantage point</note>: one must therefore avoid this composite <term>perspective</term> <note>The science of representing three-dimensional objects on a two-dimensional surface</note> and stick to the simple kind: which does not require a wall in <term>raccourci</term> <note>original: "raccourci"; the artistic technique of foreshortening, where an object appears shorter than it is to create the illusion of depth</note>, and where the wall cuts the <term>pyramides</term> <note>Leonardo’s term for the converging lines of light traveling from an object to the eye</note> which carry to the eye images <note>original: "espèces"; a term from medieval optics referring to the "likeness" or "form" of an object traveling through the air</note> that are equally distant.

Regarding articulations and joints: in what manner they increase the flesh during their flexions and extensions. Of this knowledge of great importance, I shall make a particular treatise in the description of four-footed animals, among which is man who, he too, in infancy goes on four feet.

<summary>Leonardo discusses how the curvature of the pupil affects the perception of light and size, argues for a simplified system of perspective using fixed apertures, and announces a planned treatise on the anatomy of joints in humans and animals.</summary>

<keywords>optics, pupil, perspective, foreshortening, anatomy, joints, locomotion, human development</keywords>

### 37 r.

The flight of migratory birds goes against the wind because it is less tiring and more sustainable; the bird penetrates the wind by means of an oblique movement <note>Leonardo is describing a "tacking" motion similar to a sailboat, where the bird angles its body to slice through resistance.</note>.

It is very rare for a bird to fly in the same direction as the wind.

The bird spreads its wings wide in order to achieve greater slowness <note>By increasing surface area, the bird creates more drag to act as a brake.</note>.

### 37 v.

The bird that descends makes itself all the more rapid by tucking in its wings and its tail.

### 38 r.

There is as much force involved in moving the air against a stationary object as there is in moving an object against stationary air <note>This is a foundational observation of relative motion, a precursor to modern laws of physics regarding aerodynamic lift.</note>.

### 38 v.

The movement of convolution <note>circular or spiraling flight</note> performed by birds when they rise upon the wind comes from the fact that one wing enters into the wind while the other maintains itself within the line of the wind.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of avian flight, noting how birds use wind resistance to climb and how they manipulate their wing surface area to control speed. He correctly identifies the principle of relative motion between an object and the air.</summary>

### 79 v.

The first part of painting consists of the relief <note>The illusion of three-dimensional depth and volume on a flat surface.</note> of bodies and that the background appears with its proper distance. One produces this painting by means of three perspectives: the diminution <note>reduction in size</note> of figures, the diminution of quantities, and the diminution of colors. Of these three perspectives, the first has its origin in the eye; the other two in the air interposed between the objects and the eye. The second part lies in appropriate and varied actions, according to the statures, so that the men do not appear to be brothers. <note>Leonardo emphasizes the importance of variety in human poses and proportions to achieve realism.</note>

### 80 r.

The air never possesses the speed of its motor <note>The object or force causing the air to move.</note>; the dust raised by a galloping horse will soon turn back with a swirling motion.

# MANUSCRIPT G

*Octavo <note>A book size where each sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves, resulting in a small, portable volume.</note> similar to manuscripts E and F, 96 leaves; leaves 67, 18, 19, and 31 are missing.*
*Significant sections on painting and plant organography <note>The study of the structure and development of the organs of plants.</note>.*
*G. de Venturi <note>Giovanni Battista Venturi (1746–1822), the physicist who first brought Leonardo's scientific writings to modern attention.</note>, X^a d'Oltrocchi <note>Refers to Baldassarre Oltrocchi, a former prefect of the Ambrosian Library who cataloged these works.</note>.*
*In 1515, Leonardo is in Pavia, then Bologna, and sees Milan for the last time.*
*He can already be considered a pensioner <note>A recipient of a royal stipend or financial support.</note> of Francis I.*

---

### Inside of the cover.

The magnificent Giuliano de' Medici departed on the 9th day of January 1515 at dawn (Giuliano, the third son of Lorenzo <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" de' Medici.</note> and brother of Leo <note>Pope Leo X.</note>) to be married in Savoy (to Philiberta, daughter of Philip, Count of Bresse and Duke of Savoy, and sister of Louise of Savoy).

And on this day news reached us of the death of the King of France, Louis XII, who died on January 1, 1514 <note>Though Louis XII died in 1515 by the modern calendar, Leonardo or the transcriber may be using the "incarnation" style calendar where the new year began in March, or simply recording the date as it appeared in contemporary reports.</note>.

-> Recto <-

At Saint Mary’s in the valley of Ravagnate <note>original: "Ranvagnan"; likely referring to the Val Ravagnate in Brianza</note> in the mountains of Brianza <note>original: "Bryontia"</note> are chestnut poles of 9 braccia <term>braccia: a Renaissance unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or a "cubit"</term> and of 14; and they cost 5 lire per 100 poles of 9 braccia.

I learned by percussion <note>the physical impact of one body against another</note> that the incident motion <note>the initial movement toward a surface</note> surpasses the reflected motion. At Varallo Pombia <note>original: "Varollo Piombio"</note>, near Sesto on the Ticino river, the quinces are white, large, and hard.

The bases of trees have a globular surface caused by their roots which carry nourishment to the tree; these surfaces have bark with crevices and dried-out hollows, because the nourishment reaches them poorly.

-> Verso <-

Monte Bracco above Saluzzo, beyond the Carthusian monastery <note>original: "Chartreuse"</note>, one mile from Mount Viso, has a quarry of stratified stone <note>original: "pierre lamellée"</note>, which is as white as Carrara marble and harder than porphyry <term>porphyry: a very hard igneous rock, typically purple-red, prized in antiquity</term>. My colleague Master Benedetto has promised to send me a small slab of it for grinding colors. January 5, 1511.

# THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

<page-num>319</page-num>

Arottinio <note>original: "Arottinio"; possibly a proper name or a reference to a specific location or person</note> has some of these that are ash-colored and very hard.

The <term>heart</term> <note>the central organ of the circulatory system</note> is a principal <term>muscle</term> of strength; it is much more powerful than the other <term>muscles</term> (*fig. heart in foreshortening*).

I have written down the position of the <term>muscles</term> that descend from the lower <term>valve</term> <note>the structures that control blood flow</note> to the tip of the <term>heart</term>, and the graduation of those that originate at the tip of the <term>heart</term> and go to its base (*fig. extended heart*).

The <term>auricles of the heart</term> <note>original: "oreilles du cœur"; the upper chambers or appendages of the heart</note> are the outposts of this <term>heart</term>, which receive the <term>blood</term> when it escapes from this <term>ventricule</term> <note>the lower pumping chamber</note>, from the beginning to the end of the <term>contraction</term> <note>original: "serrement"; the systolic squeezing of the heart</note>, because if this <term>blood</term> did not partially escape, the <term>heart</term> would not be able to contract.

Never can a <term>pyramid</term> with a <term>triangular base</term> have its three sides meet at a single <term>point</term>, otherwise that <term>point</term> would be divisible in itself, which would contradict the <term>geometric definition</term> of a <term>point</term> <note>in Euclidean geometry, a point is defined as having no parts or magnitude</note>: one of the sides can meet at the <term>point</term>, but the three sides could not do so without destroying its <term>triangular figure</term>.

<summary>Leonardo describes the muscular power and valvular structure of the heart, explaining how the auricles facilitate contraction, and concludes with a geometric proof regarding the nature of a point and a triangular pyramid.</summary>

Therefore, you, the painter, when you depict trees from up close, remember that being beneath the tree, you will happen to see the leaves from both the front and the back <note>Leonardo often uses this direct "you, painter" address to offer practical advice on observing nature</note>.

On the front, they will appear more azure <note>original: "azurées"; referring to the blue tint caused by atmospheric perspective or the reflection of the sky</note> the more you see them in <term>foreshortening</term> <note>original: "raccourci"; the visual distortion where an object appears shorter than it is when viewed from a specific angle</note>. A single leaf will sometimes present one part from the front and another from the back; this is why you must render it with two distinct colors.

->3 v.<-

The lights that illuminate <term>opaque</term> <note>objects that do not allow light to pass through them</note> bodies are of four kinds: universal, like the light of the atmosphere that lies before our horizon; particular, such as the light from the sun, a window, a door, or any other specific opening; the third is reflected light; there is a fourth that passes through <term>translucent</term> things such as cloth or paper, but not transparent in the manner of glass, crystals, and <term>diaphanous</term> <note>completely clear or translucent</note> bodies. These latter materials create the same effect as if nothing at all were positioned between the shadowed object and the light source that illuminates it.

->4 r.<-

When one foliage is behind another,

lacks dignity by doing one thing well and another poorly, like many who study the measured and proportioned nude (*standardized model* <note>original: "poncif" — a stencil or repetitive pattern; Leonardo uses it here to criticize artists who rely on a single "ideal" template rather than observing nature</note>) and do not seek out its variety: for a man can be well-proportioned whether he be fat, thin, or average. He who does not take these varieties into account makes his figures as if they were stamped out; they all appear to be sisters, a thing which deserves the greatest blame.

### *On the order for becoming universal.*

It is an easy thing for one who knows how to represent man to become universal, since all terrestrial animals have a similarity of muscles, nerves, and bones, and vary only in length or thickness, as will be demonstrated in the anatomy <note>Leonardo planned a comprehensive treatise on comparative anatomy, comparing the structures of humans to those of animals like horses</note>. There are the water animals which are very diverse: for them, I will not persuade painters to follow a single rule because they are of infinite variety, as are insects.

->6 r.<-

For trees seen from below, against the light, with one behind the other, the furthest part of the first will be transparent and bright, and it will have its field <note>The background or visual context</note> in the dark part of the second tree.

<summary>Leonardo argues that a true artist must master the variety of the human form rather than repeating a single "ideal" type, and suggests that understanding human anatomy is the key to depicting all land animals.</summary>

### 6 v.

*Representation of the deluge.* <note>original: "Figuration du déluge" — Leonardo often wrote these "prescriptions" for paintings, detailing how a complex scene should be visualized to achieve maximum realism and emotional impact.</note>

The air was darkened by the heavy rain; it fell obliquely under the transverse pressure of the winds, making waves in the air just as dust does, with the difference that this flood was crossed by the lines made by the falling drops of water. Its color partook of that of the lightning which cleaves and tears the clouds, and the flashes struck, opening the great lakes of the flooded valleys; and at the bottom, one could see the bent greenery.

Neptune <note>The Roman god of the sea</note> appeared in the midst of the waters with his trident; Aeolus <note>The ruler of the winds in classical mythology</note> was seen with his winds dragging along floating, uprooted grasses mingled with the immense waves. The horizon, along with the entire hemisphere, was confused and inflamed by the flashes of incessant thunder. One saw men wandering, and birds filling the tall trees still sheltered from the rising tide, and they formed hills that surround the great chasms.

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, deluge, Neptune, Aeolus, winds, flood, lightning, thunder, chasms</vocab>

### 8 r.

->On the error of those who use practice<-
->without science.<-

Those who fall in love with practice without science <note>Leonardo uses "science" (scientia) to mean certain, demonstrable knowledge based on principles.</note> are like pilots who board a ship without a rudder or a compass, and who never know for certain where they are going.

Practice must always be founded upon sound theory, of which perspective is the guide and the gateway: without it, nothing can be achieved in any genre of painting.

Young plants have more transparent leaves and smoother bark than old ones.

The shadows of plants are never black, because of the air which disperses the darkness <note>Leonardo refers to "atmospheric perspective," where the air itself holds light and prevents shadows in nature from being absolute black.</note>.

### 8 v.

<term>Simple perspective</term> <note>Leonardo refers here to linear perspective on a flat surface parallel to the viewer's eye</note> is that which is created by art upon a surface that is equally distant from the eye in every one of its parts.

Nature has placed the leaves of the outermost branches in such a way that the sixth leaf is always above the first, and so on in sequence. <note>This is one of the earliest recorded observations of phyllotaxy, the mathematical arrangement of leaves around a stem.</note> The branch, or the fruit budding in the following year from the "eye" <note>the bud or axil</note> which is above, in contact with the base of the leaf, receives the water that descends when the drop falls into the concavity of that eye; and one branch does not shade or cover the other, because the branches grow in different directions, and only the sixth grows directly above the first, and at a significant distance above it.

### 19 r.

On the judgment you must make of a work of painting. You must first consider the figures: whether they have the necessary <term>relief</term> <note>the illusion of three-dimensional depth created through light and shadow</note> for their position and the required light, and that the shadows are not identical in the center and at the edges of the composition.

It is one thing to be surrounded by shadows, and another to have them on only one side; those in the center represent the first case, as they are shaded by the dark figures placed between them. Those figures are shaded on only one side which are placed between the light and the composition; for either the light is not seen, but the composition "sees" it and the darkness of the composition is represented there; or the composition is not seen and the light emerges from it and is represented with clarity. <note>Leonardo is describing complex lighting scenarios where figures in the middle of a group are shaded by their neighbors, whereas figures at the edge interact directly with the primary light source or the background.</note>

Secondly, let the figures be arranged according to the intent of the composition.

Thirdly, that the character <note>the "moti mentali," or the internal emotions and purpose</note> of the figures be immediately visible.

### 19 v.

Landscapes require half-lit trees.

<summary>Leonardo outlines criteria for judging a painting, emphasizing the realistic distribution of light and shadow (relief) and the clear expression of a figure's character and role within a composition.</summary>
<keywords>painting theory, relief, light and shadow, chiaroscuro, composition, art criticism, landscape painting</keywords>

### On the smoke of cities.

Smoke is better seen toward the east; the sun appears in transparency through the forms and illuminates them, and the roofs of these houses are shadowy because their <term>obliquity</term> <note>the slant or angle of the roof</note> cannot be reached by the light. Dust is also more luminous toward the middle, according to its density.

The sun being in the east, the smoke of the cit-

### 23 v.

An important part of painting is the background, against which the outlines of natural bodies that have a convex curvature are revealed, even if the color of the bodies is identical to the background. This occurs because the convex boundaries of the bodies are not illuminated in the same way by the light that illuminates the field <note>the "field" refers to the background space behind the subject</note>, such outlines being either lighter or darker than the field.

If such boundaries are of the same color as the background, they will be poorly seen.

Such a choice is to be avoided by the good painter whose intention is to make his bodies appear in front of the field; in the aforementioned case, the opposite happens.

### Modification of vegetable color. <note>Leonardo is likely referring to the changing hues of plants and foliage under different lighting conditions</note>

Many have a desire and love for drawing

# THE MANUSCRITS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

335

->30 v.<-

On the differences between force and weight, and first concerning force.

Regarding the spring and the counterweight: the spring prevails through pyramidal power <note>original: "puissance pyramidale"; Leonardo uses this term to describe a force that increases or decreases geometrically, much like the shape of a pyramid, rather than remaining constant.</note>; but the counterweight possesses a composite power that is partly cylindrical and partly pyramidal. The cylindrical power pulls with an equal force at the beginning and at the end; the pyramidal power—as if at a single moment or point, and at every degree of movement and time—acquires magnitude and speed, provided its movement is free and rapid. In slow movement, caused by the weight, the pyramidal power ceases, and there remains only the cylindrical power, which is constant. <note>Leonardo is distinguishing between "cylindrical" (constant) force and "pyramidal" (accelerating or varying) force, likely observing how gravity affects falling bodies versus tension in a mechanical spring.</note>

->30 v.<-

->Ramification of plants.<- <note>The study of how branches divide and spread from the main trunk.</note>

->32 r.<-

The contours of every part of shaded bodies are distinguished with the highest degree of clarity in the sections positioned between the lights and the shadows. <note>Leonardo refers to the "terminator" line, where light transitions into shadow, as the area where the form of an object is most clearly defined to the eye.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanical differences between the tension of springs and the pull of gravity, while also noting the optimal visual conditions for defining the edges of three-dimensional forms through light and shadow.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, force, weight, physics, botany, ramification, optics, shadows, chiaroscuro</keywords>

### 32 v.

The sun gives spirit to the seedlings and life to the plants. I once attempted to leave only a single small root on a squash and to nourish it with water; this squash brought all its fruits to perfection, which amounted to sixty squashes of the long variety. I recognized, applying my mind with care to this vital phenomenon, I recognized that the dew of the night, through its moisture, penetrated and nourished—by way of the attachment of the large leaves—the plant and its children, or the eggs <note>Leonardo uses the term "eggs" metaphorically to describe the seeds or the early stages of the fruit's development.</note> which are meant to produce its children.

### 33 r.

Therefore you, painter, who does not possess such rules to escape the blame of those who observe them, represent everything according to nature and do not despise study, as those greedy for gain <note>A reference to commercial artists who prioritize speed and profit over the slow, careful observation of natural laws.</note> are wont to do.

### 33 v.

Remember to position your figures observing that light and shadow are of one kind in a dark place, and of another kind for a bright place.

<summary>Leonardo describes a botanical experiment proving that plants can draw nourishment from dew through their leaves and admonishes painters to prioritize the diligent study of nature over financial gain.</summary>

->34 r.<-

The sun is hot in itself by its nature, since our eye cannot withstand its brilliance. Furthermore, its rays reflected by <term>concave</term> <note>The source manuscript uses the word "convaves," likely a typographical error for "concaves."</note> mirrors prove it: no created thing can resist the heat of such a <term>percussion</term> <note>In Leonardo’s physical theory, "percussion" refers to the forceful impact of a ray or object against a surface.</note> from a reflected ray. And if you say that the mirror, which is cold, casts warm rays, I answer you that the ray comes from the sun.

### 35 v.

### 36 v.

Almost all the straight parts of plants curve, turning their convex side toward the South; and their branches are longer, thicker, and more numerous to the South than to the North. This happens because the sun draws the moisture toward the surface of the plant that is closest to it. <note>This is an early observation of phototropism, the tendency of plants to grow toward a light source.</note>

### 37 r.

The contours of bodies are the smallest of all things.
The contour is a surface which is neither part of the body, nor part of the air, but a medium interposed between the air and the body.
The lateral contour is the limit of the surface, a line of invisible thickness; you therefore, painter, do not surround bodies with lines. <note>Leonardo is arguing against the use of harsh outlines in painting, advocating instead for "sfumato," where edges are softened to mimic how the human eye perceives depth and light.</note>

<summary>Leonardo records observations on the growth patterns of trees influenced by sunlight and provides a theoretical definition of contours, advising painters to avoid hard outlines.</summary>

Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman author of Natural History</note> attributes this saltiness to the fact that once every sweet part is removed, the harsh part remains. It is also said that saltiness is a perspiration of the earth.

### 49 r.

Words that do not satisfy the ear of the listener give him boredom or grief, and you will see the expression of this many times in numerous yawnings. You who speak before men whose goodwill you seek, when you see such effects of impatience, shorten or change your reasoning: if you do otherwise, you will earn hatred and enmity instead of the desired grace.

If you wish to know what someone delights in without hearing him speak, change your reasoning several times; and that which makes him attentive without yawning, movement of eyebrows, and similar actions—be certain that the thing you are speaking of is that which delights him.

<summary>Leonardo concludes his notes on the origins of salt in nature, citing Pliny the Elder, and provides practical advice on reading body language and social cues during public discourse.</summary>

->53 r.<-

### Use of the Template. <note>The original term "Sagoma" refers to a template, mold, or gauge used for shaping or polishing curved surfaces, such as mirrors.</note>

Whether the Template be of Venus (copper) or of Jupiter (tin) or of Saturn (lead) and often cast back into the womb of its mother, let it be used with emery; let the template be of Venus and Jupiter, ensuring that the Venus escapes from it (1). <note>original: "Vénus s'en échappe"</note>

This means that the Template presses upon the object being shaped <note>original: "sagomé"</note> with a perpendicular weight, and thus the center of the object in revolution will not be worn away for lack of weight upon it; furthermore, the polishing agent will have something to receive and support it.

---

->53 v.<-

Pictorial perspective has three parts: the quantitative diminution of bodies at various distances; the attenuation of their colors; and the loss of definition of the figures and contours at various distances. <note>This refers to what we now call linear perspective, color perspective, and atmospheric or "vanishing" perspective.</note>

Perspective operates at a distance through two opposing pyramids; one with its apex in the eye <note>Leonardo conceptualizes the field of vision as a pyramid or cone where the point meets the pupil.</note>

---

(1) Leonardo is mocking alchemical language here. <note>Alchemists used planetary names as symbols for metals; Leonardo uses this jargon to describe a practical metallurgical alloy for tools.</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes the technical use of metal templates and weights for polishing curved objects, followed by a foundational definition of the three branches of pictorial perspective.</summary>
<keywords>Sagoma, template, metallurgy, alchemy, pictorial perspective, optics, visual pyramid, polishing techniques</keywords>

--- Page 369 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's discussion on the geometry of vision, specifically the "pyramids" of perspective mentioned at the end of the previous page, before transitioning to brief notes on naval speed and mechanics.</meta>

<page-num>345</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

and the base distant as far as the horizon; the second with the base at the side of the eye and the apex at the horizon. <note>Leonardo describes two ways of visualizing space: one where the "pyramid" of sight expands from the eye to the horizon, and another where parallel lines converge toward a single point on the horizon.</note>

The first applies to universality, embracing the quantities of bodies, such as a vast landscape seen through a narrow cellar window. The second applies to a particularity which appears smaller the further it recedes from the eye.

The surface of a body partakes of the color of the body that illuminates it.

And also of the color of the air that is interposed between the eye and that body—that is to say, of the color of the transparent medium interposed between the object and the eye. <note>This is a core principle of "atmospheric perspective," where distant objects appear bluer or hazier because of the volume of air between them and the viewer.</note>

Between colors of the same quality, the second <insert>the more distant one</insert> will not be of the same value. This comes from the multiplication of the color inherent to the interposed medium.

From a distant figure, one first loses the extremities and the minute parts.

### 72 v.

## Falling bodies.

### 58 v.

**Joke:**
Someone, wishing to prove by the authority of Pythagoras that he had previously been in this world, and because he was not allowed to finish his reasoning, said: "As proof, I remember that back then you were a miller."
<note>Leonardo refers here to the Pythagorean doctrine of metempsychosis, or the transmigration of souls, which was a frequent subject of Renaissance wit.</note>

His interlocutor, stung to the quick, agreed; saying that as proof, he himself remembered that this man was the donkey who used to carry his flour.

->Truth alone was the daughter of time.<-
<note>original: "La vérité seule fut fille du temps." This is one of Leonardo’s most famous philosophical aphorisms, reflecting his belief that nature and time eventually reveal objective truths over human dogma.</note>

**Joke:**
A painter was asked why, if he made such beautiful figures—which were dead things—he had made his children so ugly.
<note>This is a classic Renaissance anecdote (often attributed to Giotto) that highlights the distinction between the idealized perfection of art and the messy reality of nature.</note>

Aristotle says that if a power moves a
body through a certain space in a certain time, the same
power will move half of that body, twice,
in the same time. <note>Leonardo is referencing Aristotle's *Physics*, specifically the laws of proportion regarding force, mass, and velocity.</note> Therefore the mil-

than the weight of one ounce, so would be less
the quantity of air opposed to its course.

### 68 v.

To test if a man has a sound judge-
ment of the nature of weights, ask him at
which point one must cut one of the two
equal arms of a balance, so that the severed part,
attached to the end of what remains, would
counter-balance its opposite arm; and if he
gives you the position, he is a poor mathemati-
cian, for the thing is impossible. <note>Leonardo is posing a riddle of static equilibrium. Because the center of gravity of the combined "severed part and remainder" shifts, it can never perfectly balance the original intact arm under these specific conditions.</note>

That part of the body will be most illuminated which is struck by the light ray at the most equal angles. <note>Leonardo is describing the principle of the angle of incidence: a surface appears brightest when light hits it perpendicularly—forming two "equal" 90-degree angles—rather than at an oblique slant.</note>

### 78 v., 79 r.

Every year, when the branches of trees have reached their maturity, they all together form the thickness of their trunk. <note>This is a famous observation now known as "Leonardo’s Rule." He proposed that the total cross-sectional area of all the branches of a tree at any given height is equal to the cross-sectional area of the trunk or the main branch from which they grew.</note>

### 79-80 r.

In painting, one must consider the eye, the position of the object, and the light. <note>These three elements form the basis of Leonardo's theory of perspective and "chiaroscuro" (the treatment of light and shade).</note>

# MANUSCRIPT D

->DATE VERY UNCERTAIN FROM 1490 TO 1516<-
->QUARTO OF 20 PAGES 25-16 IN ORIGINAL BINDING<-
->ON THE FRONT OF THE FIRST BLANK LEAF<-
->THERE IS A CAPITAL S<-

*No notebook of Leonardo's presents such unity: a true monograph on the eye; it is evidently a final draft of long research. Only a specialist could properly comment on this treatise on optics, yet everyone will recognize in it the dark room <note>camera obscura</note> of photography.*

*In 1516, Leonardo settled at the Château of Clos Lucé <note>originally "Cloux"</note>, in Amboise.*

---

## Folio 1 recto

### *On the eye.*

Nature does not have uniformity of power in the visual faculty, but she gives to this faculty a greater power toward its center so as not to contradict the law which dictates that all other powers aug-

<summary>This page introduces Manuscript D, Leonardo da Vinci's focused monograph on the anatomy and optics of the eye, likely compiled during his final years in France.</summary>
<keywords>Manuscript D, Leonardo da Vinci, optics, anatomy of the eye, camera obscura, Clos Lucé, Amboise, Renaissance science</keywords>

--- Page 394 ---
<meta>This page continues the transcription of Manuscript D, focusing on Leonardo da Vinci's theories on the eye's anatomy and the physics of light. It follows the introductory text from folio 1 recto regarding the concentration of natural powers.</meta>

<page-num>370</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

ment toward their center; this is seen in the act of percussion <term>percussion: the impact or force of one body striking another</term>, in the supports of the arms of a balance where the weight, as it moves closer to the center, diminishes its gravity; it is seen in columns, walls, and pillars, it is seen in heat and in all natural phenomena.

Nature has made the surface of the pupil <note>Leonardo frequently uses the term "prunelle" (pupil) to refer to the cornea or the entire front of the eye</note> convex, so that surrounding objects can reflect their likenesses with wider angles, which would not happen if the eye were flat.

# THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

natural action occurs in the simplest manner and in the shortest possible time.

The face of the mirror is like someone looking at you, whose left eye faces your right eye, just as letters do when they are printed or as wax taking the impression of a seal.

->4 v.<- <note>Verso of the 4th leaf.</note>

The entire pupil possesses the visual faculty, which is spread throughout every point.

->5 r.<- <note>Recto of the 5th leaf.</note>

Nocturnal birds see better at night than during the day: this is due to the increase and decrease of their pupils.

The human pupil doubles its diameter at night; that of the eagle-owl <note>original: "duc"</note> or the tawny owl <note>original: "chat-huant"</note> increases ten times as much, which is equivalent to 100 times the area of the daytime pupil.

Beyond this, the ventricle located in the brain, called the *imprensiva* <note>The "imprensiva" was Leonardo’s term for the part of the brain that receives and processes sensory impressions before they reach the "common sense."</note>, is more than ten times the size of the human eye, while the pupil represents less than a thousandth part of it.

This *imprensiva* of man is like a large hall receiving light through a small window <note>original: "baie"</note>.

<summary>Leonardo compares the lateral reversal of mirrors to printing and seals, then analyzes how the massive dilation of nocturnal birds' pupils allows them to see in the dark compared to the human eye.</summary>
<keywords>optics, reflection, pupil dilation, nocturnal birds, imprensiva, neuroanatomy, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 397 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of the mechanics of the eye, specifically focusing on the relationship between the aperture of the pupil and the perceived size of objects, as well as the pupil's reflexive response to light levels.</meta>

# THE MANUSCRIPTS OF LEONARDO DA VINCI

If you make a small hole in a piece of paper and bring it close to the eye and look at a star, only a small part of the pupil operates and perceives the star with a vast space of sky in the distance. If you make another hole in the paper, you will see the same star with the other eye, and it will appear large to you. <note>Leonardo is describing how a pinhole aperture reduces the "irradiation" or glare of a star, making it appear as a tiny point compared to the larger appearance it has when viewed with the naked eye.</note> Thus, you see the same star twice with your two eyes, once large and the other small.

From this it follows that large pupils see little in the daylight, as the overabundance of light prevents vision. <note>Leonardo correctly observes that "glare" or excessive light can overwhelm the eye's ability to form a clear image.</note>

->5 v.<- <note>Verso of the 5th leaf.</note>

Nature protects the eye from excessive light by contracting the pupil, and in various degrees of darkness, she widens the pupil. <note>original: "prunelle"</note> Nature acts like someone who closes a shutter <note>original: "volet"</note> against too much light and opens it at night.

->6 r.<- <note>Recto of the 6th leaf.</note>

If you look up, you will see many stars that appear very small, although in reality they are much larger than the earth,

<summary>Leonardo describes an optical experiment using pinholes to show how aperture size affects the perceived size of stars, and explains the pupil’s biological reflex to light levels using the analogy of a window shutter.</summary>

->79 r.<-

False definition of the straight and the curved. <note>Leonardo often critiqued traditional Euclidean definitions to find more practical or "natural" explanations.</note>

->79 v.<-

The circle is a parallel figure because all the straight lines drawn from the center to the circumference are equal and fall upon the circumferential line between equal and right spherical angles. <note>Leonardo is describing the radius of a circle and how it meets the arc at a perpendicular angle.</note>

->80 r.<-

The circle is similar to a rectangular parallel <note>meaning a rectangle</note> made of a quarter of its diameter and its entire circumference, or otherwise of half of the diameter and the periphery <note>original: "périphérie"</note>. <note>This is Leonardo's attempt at "squaring the circle" by expressing the area of a circle as a rectangle where the sides are derived from the radius and circumference.</note>

->80 v.<-

Lines of the circle.

->81 r.<-

The hair of an ox <note>original: "soie du bœuf," likely referring to a stiff hair or bristle</note> placed in still water in summer regains sense, life, and movement, fear and flight, and suffers. The proof of this is that when squeezing it, it twists and unties itself. Put it back in the water, it takes flight and saves itself from danger. <note>This reflects a common Renaissance belief in spontaneous generation, where inanimate objects like horsehair were thought to turn into worms or eels when left in water.</note>

<summary>Leonardo defines the geometric properties of the circle and its area before recording a folk-scientific observation about ox hair seemingly coming to life in water.</summary>

->118 v.<-

To see the effect of the <term>pupil</term> <note>original: "prunelle"; refers here to the opening of the eye</note>, have a similar thing made in <term>crystal</term> <note>Leonardo frequently suggested using glass or crystal models to simulate the aqueous humors of the eye.</note>.

->119 r.<-

Structure of the <term>eye</term>.

->119 v. and 120 r.<-

Illusions of <term>optics</term>.

->120 v.<-

If the <term>eye</term> is in the middle of the path of two horses running in parallel, it will appear as though they are running toward one another, because the images that are imprinted upon the <term>eye</term> move toward the superficial center of the <term>pupil</term>.

<summary>This page contains Leonardo's notes on the toxic properties of distilled blood and his pioneering experiments in optics, including a proposal for a crystal model of the eye to study visual perception.</summary>
<keywords>optics, anatomy of the eye, visual illusions, distillation, chemistry, perspective, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

->121 recto and verso<-

Flight of birds. <note>Leonardo’s study of avian flight was foundational to his designs for flying machines, as he sought to understand the mechanical principles of lift and balance.</note>

->122 recto and verso, 123.<-

Stars.

->123 verso, 124 recto.<-

Perspective of movement. <note>This refers to how the appearance of objects changes as they or the observer move through space.</note>

->124 verso, 125 recto.<-

Spherical perspective. <note>A complex form of drawing that accounts for the curvature of the visual field, rather than using a flat linear plane.</note>

->125 verso, 126, 127.<-

Optics.

->128 recto.<-

Proportions of water.

->128 verso.<-

->John of the Red-Earth and Robbia.<- <note>original: "Johannes Rubricata et Robbia." Leonardo is likely referencing alchemical or chemical substances; "Rubricata" refers to red earth or ochre, while "Robbia" refers to the madder plant used for red dye.</note>
